## Verse 2:18

> They are ˹wilfully˺ deaf, dumb, and blind, so they will never return ˹to the Right Path˺

**THE THREE CLOSINGS**

The verse gathers the three faculties by which a person receives the guidance and names what has happened to each of them in the hypocrites: they are deaf, dumb, blind. Ibn ʿAbbās read the three words of the verse as three closings of the one heart: deaf, dumb, blind, he said, means they do not hear the guidance, they do not see it, and they do not understand it. The deafness is to the hearing of the revelation, the dumbness is to the speaking of the truth they do not believe, and the blindness is to the signs that are seen. The three words stand without a verb, as a description of their settled state, and the verse closes on the consequence of the three: they will not return. The return renders rajʿah, the turning back to the guidance they left, and the negation is stated for the ones whose faculties have been closed in all three directions at once.

The early scholars read the three closings as the completion of the sealing described in the passage of the open disbelievers, now applied to the hypocrites. The open disbeliever was sealed in his heart and his hearing and covered in his sight, and the hypocrite is deaf, dumb and blind, and the two descriptions meet at the same end. The verse is also the pivot of the parables: the first parable was of the fire and its going out, and the second parable, which follows, is of the rain and its darkness, and between them stands this verse with its three negations. The Book uses the same three closings of the ones it has destined for the Fire: (7:179 — **“They have hearts they do not understand with, eyes they do not see with, and ears they do not hear with. They are like cattle. In fact, they are even less guided!”**).

**THE REPORTS OF THE CLOSINGS**

The early reports give the three closings their range. Ibn ʿAbbās said, in the report of Ibn Isḥāq through ʿIkrimah or Saʿīd ibn Jubayr: deaf, dumb, blind — from the good; and in the report of ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭalḥah from him: they do not hear the guidance and do not see it and do not understand it. And al-Suddī's wide chain read the dumb as the mute. And the closing of the return is read in two registers: Qatādah said, they will not return — that is, they do not repent and do not remember; and al-Suddī's wide chain said: they will not return to Islam. And a report from Ibn ʿAbbās, in the chain of Ibn Isḥāq, read it: they will not return to the guidance nor to the good, and they will not find salvation while they are upon what they are upon — and al-Ṭabarī sets that report aside: Allah informed of the people that they do not return, from their purchase of the misguidance with the guidance, to the seeking of the guidance and the seeing of the truth, without confining that of their state to a time rather than a time or a condition rather than a condition; and this report confines it to the time of their standing upon their affair, and gives them the way of the return from it — and that of the interpretations is a claim without validity, with no indication for it from the apparent nor from a report by which the proof stands. The reading of the verse is the raising — deaf, dumb, blind — and it is not for anyone to oppose the drawings of the codices of the Muslims, and had it been read with the accusative it would have been a reading opposing their drawing.

---

## Verse 2:19

> Or ˹those caught in˺ a rainstorm from the sky with darkness, thunder, and lightning. They press their fingers into their ears at the sound of every thunder-clap for fear of death. And Allah encompasses the disbelievers ˹by His might˺

**THE SECOND PARABLE: THE STORM**

The second parable is introduced with the word for another, aw, and it is the likeness of the rainstorm from the sky, in which are darknesses and thunder and lightning. The hypocrites are described as putting their fingers in their ears from the thunderclaps, from the fear of death, and the verse closes with the statement that God encompasses the disbelievers. The parable gathers the three elements of the revelation as it falls on the hypocrites: the darknesses are the doubts and the disbelief, the thunder is the warnings that shake them, and the lightning is the lights of guidance that flash and disappear. The hypocrites are caught in the storm: they cannot escape it, and they cannot benefit from it, and they do what the man in the storm does — they press their fingers into their ears against the thunder, fearing death.

The early scholars read each element of the parable in turn. Ibn Masʿūd and Ibn ʿAbbās, with a number of other Companions, confirmed that the word rendered rainstorm, ṣayyib, is the rain, and the same was said by Abū al-ʿĀliyah, Mujāhid, Saʿīd ibn Jubayr, ʿAṭāʾ, al-Ḥasan, Qatādah, al-Suddī and al-Rabīʿ ibn Anas; al-Ḍaḥḥāk said it is the clouds, but the received reading is the rain that comes down with darkness, thunder and lightning. The rain is the Qurʾān, which is life for the earth and for the hearts, and the darknesses, thunder and lightning are what the hypocrites find in it: the darknesses are the parts they disbelieve, the thunder is the commands and the threats they fear, and the lightning is the truths they glimpse and cannot hold. The putting of the fingers in the ears is the hypocrites' turning from the revelation when it speaks of what they do not want to hear, and the fear of death is the fear that the revelation will expose them and kill their standing. The verse closes on the encompassing of God: He encompasses the disbelievers, and the word muḥīṭ carries both the knowledge and the power, so that there is no escape from what surrounds them. The Book names the same elements of the sky elsewhere, where the lightning and the heavy clouds are given as signs: (13:12 — **“He is the One Who shows you lightning, inspiring ˹you with˺ hope and fear, and produces heavy clouds”**). The parable is the complement of the first: the first was of the light that was kindled and taken, and the second is of the light that flashes and is not held.

**THE STORM IN THE REPORTS**

Al-Suddī, in the wide chain he carries from Abū Mālik and from Abū Ṣāliḥ, from Ibn ʿAbbās, and from Murrah al-Hamdānī, from Ibn Masʿūd, and from people of the Companions, gives the parable its occasion: there were two men of the hypocrites of the people of Madinah who fled from the Messenger of Allah ﷺ to the polytheists, and there befell them this rain that Allah mentioned, in which was severe thunder and thunderbolts and lightning, and every time the thunderbolts lit for them they put their fingers in their ears, from the fear that the thunderbolts would enter their hearing and kill them, and when the lightning flashed they walked in its light, and when it did not flash they did not see and stood in their place, and they kept saying, would that we had dawned so that we might come to Muḥammad and put our hands in his hand; and when they dawned they came to him and surrendered, and put their hands in his hand, and their surrender was good. So Allah made the affair of these two hypocrites who had gone out a parable for the hypocrites who were in Madinah: the hypocrites, when they attended the assembly of the Prophet ﷺ, put their fingers in their ears, from the fear of the Prophet's speech, lest something come down about them or they be mentioned with something and be killed, as those two hypocrites who had gone out put their fingers in their ears; and whenever it lit for them they walked in it — whenever their wealth grew and boys were born to them and they gained booty or gain, they walked in it.

Ibn ʿAbbās read the elements of the parable in the Qurʾān itself, in the report of ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭalḥah: or like a rainstorm from the sky — and it is the rain — He strikes its parable in the Qurʾān; in it are darknesses, He says, is trial; and thunder, He says, in it is intimidation; and the lightning — the lightning nearly snatches their sight, He says, nearly the muḥkam of the Qurʾān shows up the faults of the hypocrites; whenever it lights for them they walk in it. And the reports carry the sky's own furniture: Mujāhid, from Ibn ʿAbbās, said, the thunder is the name of an angel, and this voice of his is his glorification, and when his driving of the cloud grows severe, the cloud is shaken and is rubbed together, and the thunderbolts come out from between it; and he said, in another report from him, the lightning is an angel; and Mujāhid read the ṣayyib as the spring rain. Al-Qurṭubī gathers what was carried of the same: it is reported from Ibn ʿAbbās that he said, the thunder is a wind that is strangled between the clouds and it voices that voice; and they differed over the lightning — it is reported from ʿAlī and Ibn Masʿūd and Ibn ʿAbbās that the lightning is a whip of iron in the hand of the angel, with which he drives the clouds, and this is the apparent of the ḥadīth of al-Tirmidhī; and from Ibn ʿAbbās also, it is a whip of light in the hand of the angel with which he drives the clouds; and from him also, the lightning is an angel that appears; and the philosophers said, the thunder is the voice of the knocking together of the bodies of the clouds, and the lightning is what is struck out of their knocking together — and this is a saying that is not sound by any transmission. And Ibn ʿAbbās carries the practice of the road in the storm: he said, we were with ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb on a journey between Madinah and al-Shām, and with us was Kaʿb al-Aḥbār, and there befell us a wind and thunder and severe rain and cold, and the people feared; and Kaʿb said to me, whoever says when he hears the thunder, glory to Him whom the thunder glorifies with His praise and the angels from the fear of Him, is spared from what is in that cloud, of the cold and the thunderbolts; and I said it, and Kaʿb said it, and when we dawned and the people gathered, I said to ʿUmar, O Commander of the Faithful, it is as if we had been in other than what the people were in — and I told him the report of Kaʿb.

---

## Verse 2:20

> It is as if the lightning were about to snatch away their sight. Whenever lightning strikes, they walk in its light, but when darkness covers them, they stand still. Had Allah willed, He could have taken away their hearing and sight. Surely Allah is Most Capable of everything

**THE WALKING AND THE STANDING**

The verse completes the second parable by describing how the hypocrites live inside the storm. The lightning nearly snatches their sight: the flashes of truth are so near to taking their vision that they cannot look away, and when it gives them light they walk in it, and when it darkens upon them they stand still. Ibn ʿAbbās said of the lightning almost snatching their sight that the Qurʾān mentions nearly all of the secrets of the hypocrites, and of the walking and the standing he said, in the report of Ibn Isḥāq, that they recognise the truth and speak about it, so their speech is upright, but when they revert to disbelief they fall into confusion again — and the same was said by Abū al-ʿĀliyah, al-Ḥasan, Qatādah, al-Rabīʿ ibn Anas and al-Suddī. The walking and the standing are the two states of the hypocrite: he moves when the revelation gives him something he can use, and he stops when it demands of him what he will not give. The early scholars read the walking as the hypocrites' momentary following of the believers when there is gain in it, and the standing as their stopping when there is cost. The verse then states the power that holds the whole scene: if God had willed, He would have taken their hearing and their sight, and the verse closes on the power of God over all things.

The structure of the two parables is the argument of the passage. The first parable is of the hypocrites who touched the light and lost it — the ones who declared faith and then turned — and the second is of the hypocrites who live in the storm and cannot hold the light that flashes — the ones who never truly entered. The two parables together cover the two kinds of hypocrisy the sūrah addresses, and the passage of thirteen verses that began with the claim of belief closes on the power of God over the hearing and the sight. The same closing, on the power of God over everything, stands at the end of this sūrah's own long argument about what the hearts conceal: (2:284 — **“Whether you reveal what is in your hearts or conceal it, Allah will call you to account for it”**).

**THE TAKING AWAY AND THE POWER**

Al-Ṭabarī gives the verse its reason: Allah singled out the hearing and the sight — that He would take them away from the hypocrites rather than the other members of their bodies — because of what ran of the mention of them in the two verses, in His saying, they put their fingers in their ears from the thunderclaps, and His saying, the lightning nearly snatches their sight, whenever it lights for them they walk in it; then He followed the mention of that by stating that if He willed He would take them away from the hypocrites as a punishment upon them for their hypocrisy and their disbelief — a threat from Allah to them, as He threatened them in the verse before it with His saying, and Allah is encompassing of the disbelievers, describing Himself as the One who has power over them and over their gathering, for the letting down of His displeasure upon them and the sending down of His vengeance, and warning them by it His seizure and frightening them with His punishment, that they might fear His might and hasten to Him with repentance. Ibn ʿAbbās said, in the report of Ibn Isḥāq through ʿIkrimah or Saʿīd ibn Jubayr: if Allah had willed, He would have taken away their hearing and their sight — for what they left of the truth after the knowledge of it; and al-Rabīʿ ibn Anas read it of the hearing of the hypocrites and their sight by which they lived among the people.

The singular of the hearing beside the plural of the sights has its own argument: some of the grammarians of Kūfah said He made the hearing singular because He intended the source and aimed at the faculty, and gathered the sights because He intended the eyes; and some of the grammarians of Basrah held that the hearing, though it is one in wording, is in the meaning of the multitude, and he argued with His saying, their gaze does not return to them, and they turn the backs. Al-Ṭabarī settles it: what makes it sound with me is that in the speech there is what indicates that the multitude is intended by it, so that the one of the hearing performed the meaning of the multitude in place of its gathering — and had the like of what was done with the hearing been done with the sight, or the like of what was done with the sights been done with the hearing, of the gathering and the singular, it would have been eloquent and sound. And Allah described Himself with the power over all things in this place because He warned the hypocrites of His might and His seizure and informed them that He is encompassing of them and able to take away their hearing and their sight — so fear Me, you hypocrites, and beware of deceiving Me and My Messenger and the people of faith in Me, lest I bring down upon you My vengeance, for I am over that and over other things able; and qadīr is in the meaning of qādir, as ʿalīm is in the meaning of ʿālim, with the increase of the meaning of faʿīl over fāʿil in praise and in dispraise.

---

## Verse 2:21

> O humanity! Worship your Lord, Who created you and those before you, so that you may become mindful ˹of Him˺

**THE CALL TO ALL THREE KINDS**

The sūrah now turns from the description of the three kinds of people to the call that addresses them all, and it opens with the widest address in the Book: O humanity, worship your Lord. The call is to worship, ʿibādah, and it is the standing word of the revelation: the word that gathers belief, obedience, love and humility into one. Ibn ʿAbbās said, in the report of ʿIkrimah and Saʿīd ibn Jubayr, that the address O humanity, worship your Lord is made to the two parties together, the disbelievers and the hypocrites: single out your Lord, who created you and those before you; and he read the command worship your Lord as single out your Lord, setting apart the obedience and worship of your Lord from all the rest of His creation. The verse names the Lord by His first act: He who created you and those before you, and it gives the purpose of the worship: so that you may attain taqwā — and Mujāhid read so that you may become mindful as so that you may obey. The structure of the call is the structure of the sūrah: the worship is commanded, the Creator is named as the reason, and taqwā is named as the fruit, which is the same word with which the sūrah opened in the description of the guided.

The early scholars read the verse as the hinge between the description and the law. The call to worship is the call that precedes all the commands and prohibitions that follow in the sūrah, and it is addressed to the believers, the disbelievers and the hypocrites together, because the worship is the matter on which all three are tested. The verse names the creation as the ground of the worship, and the creation is described as yours and of those before you, so that the one addressed knows that his Lord is the Lord of his whole kind, and that the worship is not a new demand but the standing demand of the creation. The Book states the same call in the sūrah of the Women, which opens with the same address and the same ground: (4:1 — **“O humanity! Be mindful of your Lord Who created you from a single soul, and from it He created its mate, and through both He spread countless men and women”**).

**WHO IS ADDRESSED AND WHAT WORSHIP IS**

Al-Ṭabarī fixes the addressees of the summons: God commanded the two parties both — the party He told it will be the same for them whether they are warned or not, because of the sealing upon their hearts and their hearing, and the party that deceives God and those who believe with what its tongue shows while its heart conceals the opposite — and the rest of His creation, the accountable, with humility and submission by obedience, and with singling Him out for lordship and worship rather than the idols and the false gods, for He is the creator of them and of their fathers and forefathers before them, and the creator of their idols and false gods: the One who created you and your fathers and all creation other than you, and who is able to harm you and to benefit you — He is the one who deserves that you worship Him. And Ibn ʿAbbās said the like, except that he was reported to say of worship your Lord: single out your Lord; and Ibn Isḥāq carries from Ibn ʿAbbās, through ʿIkrimah or Saʿīd ibn Jubayr: God said, O mankind, worship your Lord, to the two parties both, of the disbelievers and the hypocrites — that is, single out your Lord, who created you and those before you. And al-Suddī's wide chain read, created you and those before you, as, created you and created those before you.

Al-Ṭabarī draws the doctrine from the summons itself: this verse is among the clearest evidence for the corruption of the saying of those who claim that imposing what cannot be borne except with God's assistance is not permissible except after God has given the one imposed upon the assistance for what He imposed upon him — for God imposed upon the two parties the worship of Him in the very place where He describes one of them with the sealing upon the heart and the hearing, and did not make the assistance a condition of the command. And of the closing of the verse, Mujāhid read, that you might be God-fearing, as, that you might obey; and al-Ṭabarī reads Mujāhid's intent as: that you might fear your Lord by obeying Him and by your desisting from your error.

---

## Verse 2:22

> ˹He is the One˺ Who has made the earth a place of settlement for you and the sky a canopy; and sends down rain from the sky, causing fruits to grow as a provision for you. So do not knowingly set up equals to Allah ˹in worship˺

**THE FOUR GIFTS AND THE ONE PROHIBITION**

The verse continues the description of the Creator begun in the previous verse, and it names four gifts in order: He made the earth a bed, and the sky a building, and He sent down water from the sky and brought forth fruits from it as provision for you. The four gifts are the standing grounds of the human life on the earth: the place to live, the roof above it, the water that comes down, and the fruit that comes up. The verse then closes on the prohibition that the gifts argue for: do not set up equals with God, and the verse adds the word while you know, to state that the one who sets up equals does so with knowledge, and the knowledge is what makes the act what it is. The standing of this prohibition in the revelation is what the two Ṣaḥīḥs record from Ibn Masʿūd: I asked the Messenger of Allah, "Which evil deed is the worst with Allah?" and he said: "To set up an equal with Allah while He alone created you."

The early scholars read the four gifts as the four proofs of the one Lord. The bed of the earth is the proof of the Creator who made it fit for life, the building of the sky is the proof of the One who raised it without pillars, the water sent down is the proof of the One who provides, and the fruits brought forth are the proof of the One who gives life to the dead earth. The four proofs are the same four grounds the sūrah will return to in the verse of the creation of the heavens and the earth, and the verse closes on the prohibition of the andād, the equals, which is the word the Book uses for the partners set up with God. The structure of the two verses is the argument of the whole revelation: the Creator is described by His acts, and the worship is commanded on the ground of the acts, and the one prohibition is stated at the end of the description. The Book states the same prohibition in the language of the creation: (6:1 — **“All praise is for Allah Who created the heavens and the earth and made darkness and light. Yet the disbelievers set up equals to their Lord ˹in worship˺”**).

**THE FURNITURE AND THE EQUALS**

The reports read the furniture one by one. Al-Suddī's wide chain said: the one who made the earth for you a carpet — it is a carpet upon which one walks, and it is the resting-place and the dwelling; and al-Rabīʿ ibn Anas read it as a resting-place; and of the sky, al-Suddī's wide chain said: the building of the sky upon the earth is in the form of a dome, and it is a roof over the earth. And the verse of the rivals carries its readings: Mujāhid said, rivals — that is, equals; and al-Suddī's wide chain said: equals of men, whom you obey in disobedience to God — and the equal, in this reading, is not a carved thing but a followed one; and Ibn ʿAbbās, in the report of Ibn Isḥāq, gives the verse its occasion: that was revealed concerning the two parties both, of the disbelievers and the hypocrites, and God intends by His saying, do not set up rivals to God while you know — do not associate with God other than Him, of the rivals that do not benefit and do not harm, while you know that there is no lord for you who provides for you other than Him, and you already know that what the Messenger calls you to, of His singling-out, is the truth in which there is no doubt.

Al-Ṭabarī extends the while you know to its full width: what was with the Arabs of the knowledge of the oneness of God, and that He is the originator of creation and its creator and provider, was the like of what was with the People of the two Books of that, and there is no indication in the verse that God intended by it one of the two parties; the address came out general to all people, since He challenged people all of them with His saying, O mankind, worship your Lord — so its interpretation is what Ibn ʿAbbās and Qatādah said: that He intends every accountable one who knows the oneness of God and that there is no partner for Him in His creation, yet associates with Him in His worship other than Him, whoever he may be of the people, Arab or non-Arab, writer or unlettered.

---

## Verse 2:23

> And if you are in doubt about what We have revealed to Our servant, then produce a sûrah like it and call your helpers other than Allah, if what you say is true

**THE CHALLENGE IN ITS FIRST FORM**

The sūrah now turns to the doubt that stands behind the denial, and it addresses it with the challenge. The verse states its condition: if you are in doubt of what We have revealed to Our servant, then bring a sūrah like it. The challenge is given in its shortest form here — one sūrah — and the verse calls on the doubters to bring their witnesses, other than God, if they are truthful. The early scholars read the witnesses of the verse in three ways: Ibn ʿAbbās said your witnesses means your aids; al-Suddī reported from Abū Mālik that it means your partners — some other people to help you in that, so go and seek the help of your deities to support and aid you; and Mujāhid said it means people — wise and eloquent men who will provide the testimony you seek. The word mīthl, like, is the word of the challenge, and the verse asks for the like of what has been revealed, not for something better or different, but for the like. The early scholars read the verse as the standing proof of the revelation: the Book challenges its doubters to produce its like, and the challenge has not been met, and the standing of the challenge is the standing of the Book.

The reports preserve the occasions of the challenge. The Quraysh and the Jews doubted the revelation and asked for a sign, and the verse answered them with the challenge, which is the sign that requires no sign. The verse names the Prophet as Our servant, and the word ʿabd, servant, is the standing word of the revelation for the Prophet, and it is the word that names his highest standing: the servant of God. The verse closes on the condition of truthfulness: if you are truthful, and the condition is the standing condition of the challenge — the one who claims the Book is not from God is called to prove his claim by producing its like, and the claim that cannot be proved is the claim of the one who is not truthful. The Book repeats the same challenge in the sūrah of Yūnus, against the claim that the Prophet made it up: (10:38 — **“Or do they claim, “He made it up!”? Tell them ˹O Prophet˺, “Produce one sûrah like it then, and seek help from whoever you can—other than Allah—if what you say is true!””**).

**THE CHALLENGE IN ITS OWN LANGUAGE**

Al-Ṭabarī states the verse's place in the argument: this is a proof from God for His Prophet Muḥammad ﷺ against the associators of his people among the Arabs and their hypocrites, and the disbelievers of the People of the Book and their strayers — those whose story He opened with His saying, indeed those who disbelieve — it is they whom He addresses with these verses. He is saying: if you are in doubt — that is, in uncertainty — of what We have sent down upon Our servant Muḥammad ﷺ, of the light and the proof and the verses of the Criterion, that it is from Me and that I am the one who sent it down to him, and you have not believed in it nor affirmed him in what he says, then bring a proof that repels his proof, for you know that the proof of every claimant of prophecy upon his truth is that he brings a demonstration that all creation are unable to bring the like of. And He challenged them with their own instrument, not with another's: bring a sūrah of your own speech that is its like in Arabic, since you are Arabs, and it is a speech the equal of your speech and a language resembling your language — He did not impose upon them a sūrah from a tongue other than the tongue in which the Qurʾān came down, so that they could have said, you have imposed upon us what, if we were skilled in it, we would bring, and we are unable because we are not of the people of the tongue you have imposed upon us.

Al-Qurṭubī gives the verse its history and its grammar: the intended are the associators who were challenged, for when they heard the Qurʾān they said, this does not resemble the speech of God, and we are in doubt concerning it — so the verse came down; and its connection with what precedes it is that God, when He mentioned in the first verse the indication of His oneness and His power, mentioned after it the indication of the prophethood of His Prophet, and that what he brought was not fabricated from him; and the servant means Muḥammad ﷺ, and the servant is taken from taʿabbud, which is humility; and bring is a command whose meaning is the rendering helpless, since He knew their inability; and the pronoun in of its like returns upon the Qurʾān in the saying of the majority of the scholars, among them Qatādah and Mujāhid; and it was said, it returns upon the Torah and the Gospel. And of your witnesses, He said, it means your helpers and your supporters; al-Farrāʾ said, your gods; and Ibn Kaysān answered why witnesses are summoned to a production: the meaning is, seek aid from whom you find of your scholars and bring them to witness what you produce, so that the rebuttal of all of them is more forceful in the argument against them; and Mujāhid read the witnesses as, people who witness; and if you are truthful, that is, in what you said of your ability to oppose.

---

## Verse 2:24

> But if you are unable to do so—and you will never be able to do so—then fear the Fire fuelled with people and stones, which is prepared for the disbelievers

**THE VERDICT OF THE CHALLENGE**

The verse answers the challenge before it is met: if you do not do it — and you will never do it — then fear the Fire. The negation is given in two forms: the conditional if you do not, and the standing statement you will never do it, and the two together state the verdict of the challenge: the like of the Book will not be produced. The early scholars read the object of the challenge accordingly: bring a sūrah like it means similar to the Qurʾān, and this is the tafsīr of Mujāhid and Qatādah, and al-Rāzī stated that it is the reading of ʿUmar, Ibn Masʿūd, Ibn ʿAbbās, al-Ḥasan al-Baṣrī and the majority of the scholars. The verse then names the consequence of the failed challenge: the Fire, whose fuel is the people and the stones, which has been prepared for the disbelievers. The fuel of the Fire is described as the people and the stones, and the early scholars read the stones as the stones of the idols that were worshipped, so that the worshippers and the worshipped are gathered into one fuel.

The verse is the pivot of the opening argument: the challenge is given, the verdict of the challenge is stated, and the consequence of the denial is named, all in one verse. The word ittaqū, fear with taqwā, is the same word with which the sūrah opened, and the verse thus returns the reader to the first word: the taqwā that was the condition of the guidance in the second verse is now commanded as the fear of the Fire. The Fire is described as prepared for the disbelievers, and the word uʿiddat, prepared, states that it is made ready in advance, and the preparation is the standing argument of the verse: the Fire is not a possibility but a prepared fact. The Book describes the same Fire in the sūrah of the Prohibition, with the same fuel and its keepers: (66:6 — **“O believers! Protect yourselves and your families from a Fire whose fuel is people and stones, overseen by formidable and severe angels, who never disobey whatever Allah orders—always doing as commanded”**).

**THE NEWS OF NEVER AND THE STONES OF THE FIRE**

The two clauses of the verse are read by the early scholars each on its own. Al-Ṭabarī reads the first: His saying, then if you do not, means, if you did not bring a sūrah of its like, while you and your partners upon it and your helpers combined together, and your inability and the inability of all My creation of it became clear to you by your testing and your trial, and you knew that it is from Me, and then you remained upon the denial of it; and His saying, and you will never do it — that is, you will never bring a sūrah of its like, ever. And Qatādah said: you will not have power over that and will not bear it; and Ibn ʿAbbās said, in the report of Ibn Isḥāq through ʿIkrimah or Saʿīd ibn Jubayr: then the truth has been made plain to you.

And the fire is described by its fuel. Al-Ṭabarī reads the command: fear the fire, he says, is, fear that you enter the fire by your denial of My Messenger in what he brought you from Me that it is from My revelation and My sending down, after it has become plain to you that it is My book and from Me, and after the proof has been established upon you that it is My speech and My revelation, by your inability and the inability of all My creation to bring the like of it; then He described the fire with which He warned them and informed them that people are its fuel and that the stones are its fuel. And Ibn Masʿūd said, in the report that reaches him through ʿAbd al-Razzāq from Sufyān ibn ʿUyaynah, from Miṣʿar, from ʿAbd al-Malik al-Zarrād, from ʿAmr ibn Maymūn: its fuel is the people and the stones — the stones of brimstone; God made them as He willed. The verse binds the two arguments together: the proof of the message stands in the tongue, and the warning of the fire stands behind the denial of the proof — and the fire whose fuel is men and stones is the fitting counterpart of a challenge that no tongue could meet.

---

## Verse 2:25

> Give good news ˹O Prophet˺ to those who believe and do good that they will have Gardens under which rivers flow. Whenever provided with fruit, they will say, “This is what we were given before,” for they will be served fruit that looks similar ˹but tastes different˺. They will have pure spouses, and they will be there forever

**THE NEWS OF THE BELIEVERS**

The passage of the challenge closes on the other news, and it is the good news of the believers. The verse commands the Prophet to give good news to those who believe and do the righteous deeds: they will have gardens under which the rivers flow. The verse then describes the fruit of the gardens: whenever they are given a fruit from it, they say, this is what we were given before, and they are given its like. The verse continues with the spouses: they will have purified spouses, and the verse closes on the permanence: they will abide therein forever. The structure of the news is the complement of the warning: the Fire is prepared for the disbelievers, and the gardens are the news of the believers, and the two stand as the two ends of the argument that began with the challenge.

The early scholars read each element of the news. The gardens under which the rivers flow are the standing description of Paradise in the Book, and the rivers are described as flowing under the gardens because the water is the life of the garden. The saying of the people of Paradise, this is what we were given before, is read in two ways: that the fruits of Paradise resemble the fruits of the world in their names and their appearances, and that the people of Paradise are given the like of what they were promised in the world. Ibn ʿAbbās stated the principle of the resemblance: nothing in Paradise resembles anything in the life of this world except in name, and in another report from him: only the names are similar between what is in this life and what is in Paradise. The purified spouses are the spouses purified from the defects of the world: Ibn ʿAbbās said, purified from filth and impurity, and Mujāhid said, from menstruation — and the abiding forever is the standing condition of the news: the good of Paradise is not a passing good but a permanent one. The same news is carried in the sūrah of the Women with the same elements: (4:57 — **“As for those who believe and do good, We will admit them into Gardens under which rivers flow, to stay there for ever and ever. There they will have pure spouses, and We will place them under a vast shade”**). The verse is the first of the descriptions of Paradise in the sūrah, and the sūrah will return to it many times, in the language of the gardens and the rivers and the purified spouses and the abiding forever.

**THE SCENE OF THE FRUIT**

The reports give the scene of the fruit its chain and its detail. Al-Suddī's wide chain said of they will say, this is what we were provided with before: they are brought the fruit in Paradise, and when they look at it they say, this is what we were provided with before, in the world; and Mujāhid, from Ibn Abī Najīḥ, read it: they say, how like it is to what we had. And of they are given its like, al-Suddī's wide chain said: they are given it similar in color and in appearance, and it does not resemble it in taste; and Mujāhid said: they are given it similar, of the choice kind; and in his other report: similar in its color, differing in its taste, like the choice kind of the cucumber; and al-Rabīʿ ibn Anas read it: some of it resembles some, and the taste differs. And al-Ḥasan recited the verses of al-Baqarah, and when he came to this verse he said: have you not looked at the fruits of the world, how you reject some of them? And in that fruit of Paradise, there is nothing rejected. The recognition is the pleasure of the scene: the eye finds the known shape, and the taste finds the new, and the two together are the gift.

---

## Verse 2:26

> Surely Allah does not shy away from using the parable of a mosquito or what is even smaller. As for the believers, they know that it is the truth from their Lord. And as for the disbelievers, they argue, “What does Allah mean by such a parable?” Through this ˹test˺, He leaves many to stray, and guides many. And He leaves none to stray except the rebellious—

**THE OBJECTION AND ITS ANSWER**

The sūrah now anticipates the objection the parables will draw, and answers it before the objectors finish. In the report al-Suddī carries from Ibn ʿAbbās, Ibn Masʿūd and other Companions: when Allah gave the two examples of the hypocrites — the one who kindled a fire and the rainstorm from the sky — the hypocrites said, Allah is far more exalted than to make such examples, so Allah revealed these verses down to "it is they who are the losers." Qatādah stated the principle of the verse: Allah does not shy away from the truth when He mentions a matter as a parable, whether the matter is significant or not; when Allah mentioned the flies and the spider in His Book, the people of misguidance said, why did Allah mention these things, and this verse was revealed. The answer of the verse is the distinction between its two audiences: the believers know the parable is the truth from their Lord, and the knowledge is the knowledge of the ones who received the Book with its light, while the disbelievers ask what Allah means by such a parable. The verse gives the answer inside the objection: through the parable He leaves many to stray and guides many, so the same word raises one heart and lowers another, and the parable is the test that sorts the hearts. The straying is then restricted to its people: He leaves none to stray except the rebellious, the fāsiqūn whose own departure from obedience is what made them a place for the misguidance to land.

**NOTHING IS BENEATH THE PARABLE**

The smallness of creation is beneath nothing that Allah does, because the small is one of the clearest proofs of the One who made it. The Prophet ﷺ taught the same scale of attention in what Muslim records from ʿĀʾishah: no Muslim is harmed by a thorn, or something larger, but a good deed is written for him by it and an evil deed is erased from his record — if no affliction is too small for the reward, then no creature is too small for the parable. The Book draws its parables from the whole creation, the small of it and the great: the fly that all the invoked ones cannot create: (22:73 — **“those ˹idols˺ you invoke besides Allah can never create ˹so much as˺ a fly”**), the spider whose house is the weakest of houses: (29:41 — **“The parable of those who take protectors other than Allah is that of a spider spinning a shelter”**), and the two trees of the good word and the evil word: (14:24 — **“Do you not see how Allah compares a good word to a good tree? Its root is firm and its branches reach the sky”**). The verse is the sūrah's standing rule of reading: the parable is not measured by the size of its image but by the truth it carries, and the one who objects to the image has missed the truth.

**THE OCCASION OF THE OBJECTION**

Al-Suddī gives the verse its occasion, in the wide chain he carries from Abū Mālik and from Abū Ṣāliḥ, from Ibn ʿAbbās, and from Murrah al-Hamdānī, from Ibn Masʿūd, and from people of the Companions: when God struck these two parables for the hypocrites — His saying, their parable is like the parable of one who kindled a fire, and His saying, or like a rainstorm from the sky, the three verses — the hypocrites said: God is higher and more majestic than to strike parables like these. So God sent down: indeed, God is not ashamed to strike a parable of a gnat or what is above it, until His saying: it is they who are the losers. And al-Rabīʿ ibn Anas gives the parable its reading of the age: the gnat lives while it is hungry, and dies when it has drunk its fill; so likewise those for whom God struck this parable — when they are filled from the world with drinking, God seizes them and destroys them; and that is His saying (6:44 — **“When they became oblivious to warnings, We showered them with everything they desired. But just as they became prideful of what they were given, We seized them by surprise, then they instantly fell into despair”**).

And Mujāhid reads the verse's own working: a parable of a gnat — that is, the parables, their small and their great; the believers believe in them and know that they are the truth from their Lord, and God guides them by them and leads astray by them the corrupt — the believer knows it and believes in it, and the corrupt knows it and disbelieves in it. Al-Ṭabarī settles the occasion upon this reading: everyone who has spoken in this verse and in the meaning in which it came down has gone his own way; but the worthiest of that with the right and the most resembling the truth is what we have mentioned of the saying of Ibn Masʿūd and Ibn ʿAbbās — that the verse answered the objection of the hypocrites to the parables of the fire and the storm, and made the gnat, the smallest of what is struck, the measure of the objection and of the answer.

---

## Verse 2:27

> those who violate Allah’s covenant after it has been affirmed, break whatever ˹ties˺ Allah has ordered to be maintained, and spread corruption in the land. It is they who are the ˹true˺ losers

**THE THREE MARKS OF THE REBELLIOUS**

The verse defines the rebellious of the previous verse by three marks, and the first is the violation of the covenant of Allah after it has been affirmed. The covenant is named without specification because its kinds are several: the covenant taken from the loins of the children of Adam, when their Lord made them testify of themselves: (7:172 — **“Am I not your Lord?” They replied, “Yes, You are! We testify.”**); the covenant of the nature every soul is created on; and the covenants the messengers came to renew. In one report the verse is read of all the disbelievers, the idol worshippers and the hypocrites together: Allah took their pledge to believe in His oneness, showing them the signs that testify to His lordship, and took a covenant from them to obey His commands and refrain from His prohibitions, and His messengers came with proofs and miracles that none of the creation could produce; and the covenant was broken when they denied what was proven to them to be authentic and rejected the prophets and the books although they knew them. The second mark is breaking what Allah has ordered to be maintained, and Qatādah read it as the ties of the relatives, so the verse joins the breaking between the servant and his Lord to the breaking between the servant and his kin in one description. The third is the corruption in the land, the word the passage of the hypocrites has already carried, now returned as the standing mark of the rebellious.

The verse closes on the loss: it is they who are the losers. The loss is the sūrah's answer to the question of the objectors — what does Allah mean by such a parable — and the answer returns the verdict on the questioners: the losers are the ones who break the covenant, not the one who gives the parable. The same description of the covenant-breakers stands in the sūrah of the Thunder, with the same three marks and their end: (13:25 — **“And those who violate Allah’s covenant after it has been affirmed, break whatever ˹ties˺ Allah has ordered to be maintained, and spread corruption in the land—it is they who will be condemned and will have the worst abode”**).

**WHO BROKE THE COVENANT AND HOW**

The early scholars read the covenant in two registers. A group said: these verses came down about the disbelievers of the People of the Book and their hypocrites — they are the ones God intended by His saying, indeed those who disbelieve, and by His saying, and of mankind is the one who says we believe in God and the Last Day; and all that is in these verses is blame of them and reproach until their story ends. The covenant of God that they broke after its confirmation is what God took upon them in the Torah — of acting upon what is in it, and following Muḥammad ﷺ when he is sent, and affirming him and what he brought from their Lord; and their breaking of it is their denial of him after their knowledge of his reality, and their concealing the knowledge of that from the people after they gave God, from themselves, the covenant that they would make it plain to the people and not conceal it; and God informed that they cast it behind their backs and sold it for a small price. And al-Ṭabarī states the worthiest reading: these verses came down about the disbelievers of the scholars of the Jews who were in the place of the emigration of the Messenger of God ﷺ and near it, of the remnants of the Children of Israel, and of those who were upon their association of the people of hypocrisy whose story He told. And al-Qurṭubī gives the word its root: the naqḍ is the ruining of what you have knit together, of a building or a rope or a covenant — and the verse speaks of the covenant as of a thing knit, and the breaking of it as the unravelling of the knot.

Al-Rabīʿ reads the verse with the six traits of the hypocrites that the sūrah will return to: he said of who break the covenant of God after its confirmation, and sever what God has ordered to be joined, and spread corruption in the land — these are six traits in the people of hypocrisy: when they have the upper hand, they show all six — when they speak, they lie; when they promise, they break it; when they are entrusted, they betray; and they break the covenant of God after its confirmation, and sever what God has ordered to be joined, and spread corruption in the land; and when they are under the hand of others, they show the three — when they speak, they lie; when they promise, they break it; and when they are entrusted, they betray. The verse names the corruption by its root: it is not the mischief of the hand alone but the unravelling of the covenant, and the severing of what God ordered joined, and the spreading of corruption in the land — and the three together are the loss of the earth.

---

## Verse 2:28

> How can you deny Allah? You were lifeless and He gave you life, then He will cause you to die and again bring you to life, and then to Him you will ˹all˺ be returned

**THE QUESTION THAT ANSWERS ITSELF**

The sūrah now turns from the description of the kinds of people to the address of the disbelief itself, and it opens with a question. How can you deny Allah? The question is not a request for the manner but a denial of the possibility: how can the denial happen at all, when the one who denies is himself the proof against his denial? The verse gives the proof in four stations: you were lifeless and He gave you life, then He will cause you to die, then He will bring you to life again, and then to Him you will be returned. The first life is the bringing of the human from non-existence into existence, the death is the passage between the two lives, the second life is the resurrection, and the return is the standing before the one who gave the two lives. Ibn ʿAbbās and Ibn Masʿūd said, in the report al-Suddī carries from them with a number of the Companions: you were not a thing and He created you, then He will cause you to die, then He will give you life on the Day of Resurrection; and Mujāhid read the stations the same way, saying you were nothing when He created you, then He causes you to die the true death, then He gives you life, and that the saying of the people of the Fire — You made us lifeless twice and gave us life twice — is the same. The four stations are the whole history of the servant in one sentence, and each station is a proof that the one who performed it is the one who deserves the worship.

The verse is the sūrah's first statement of the resurrection in its full form, and the form it uses is the form the people of the Fire will use against themselves on the Day, when they confess the two deaths and the two lives: (40:11 — **“They will plead, “Our Lord! You made us lifeless twice, and gave us life twice. Now we confess our sins. So is there any way out?””**). The denial the verse questions is thus a denial the denier himself will abandon, and the question stands over the whole passage that follows as the standing argument: the one who gave the first life is able to give the second, and the one who is able to give the second is the one who will judge between the guided and the sealed.

**THE TWO DEATHS AND THE TWO LIVES**

The early scholars counted the deaths and the lives. Ibn Zayd read the verse with the covenant of the loins: God created them from the loins of Ādam when He took the covenant upon them, and recited (7:172 — **“And ˹remember˺ when your Lord brought forth from the loins of the children of Adam their descendants and had them testify regarding themselves”**). And al-Ṭabarī states the worthiest reading, from Ibn Masʿūd and Ibn ʿAbbās: you were dead — dead of mention, hidden in the loins of your fathers as drops, not known and not mentioned — so He gave you life by bringing you into being as sound humans, until you were mentioned and known and lived; then He kills you by the taking of your souls and the return of you to bones not known and not mentioned, in the interval until the Day you are raised; then He gives you life after that by the blowing of the souls into you, for the raising of the Hour and the Cry of the Resurrection; then to God you return after that — for God gives them life in their graves before their gathering, then He gathers them to the standing of the reckoning. And Ibn ʿAbbās and Ibn Masʿūd said, in what al-Qurṭubī carries: you were dead, non-existent, before you were created, so He gave you life — that is, He created you — then He kills you at the end of your terms, then He gives you life for the Resurrection.

Al-Qurṭubī gives the question its face: the how is a question of the state, and it stands in the place of the astonishment — these are of the ones who are to be wondered at, who disbelieved while the proof is established upon them; and it was asked how this address can be to the People of the Book while they did not disbelieve in God, and the answer is that when they did not affirm the matter of Muḥammad and did not believe him in what he brought, their denial of the Messenger was a denial of the One who sent him. And al-Ṭabarī gives the verse its place in the sūrah's long argument: God began, after the numbering of His favors, with the mention of our father Ādam, of what passed of His honoring him and His favors upon him, and of what He sent down upon his enemy Iblīs of His hastened punishment for the two of them, by their disobedience and their opposition to His command — and of His encompassing Ādam with His mercy when he repented and returned, and of His sending down upon Iblīs His curse in the immediate, and His preparing for him the abiding punishment in the last, when he was arrogant and refused repentance and return; warning them by it His judgment in the ones who return to Him in repentance, and His decree in the ones who are arrogant from return.

---

## Verse 2:29

> He is the One Who created everything in the earth for you. Then He turned towards the heaven, forming it into seven heavens. And He has ˹perfect˺ knowledge of all things

**THE EARTH FOR YOU, THE HEAVEN ABOVE YOU**

The argument continues with the creation, and the verse names the two great creations in order. The first is the earth: He is the One who created everything in the earth for you, and the word for you is the verse's gift to its address: the creation of what is on the earth is described as a creation for the human, and the early scholars drew from it the standing principle that the things of the earth are created for the human's use, and that what is lawful is the default of the creation until a prohibition arrives. The second creation is the heaven: then He turned towards the heaven and formed it into seven heavens, and the word turned renders istawā ilā, He turned to it with the will of the creation after the creation of the earth. The report of Ibn ʿAbbās, in the narration of ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭalḥah, states that the word then here relates to the order of the narration, not the order of the events: the earth was created before the heaven, and its spreading out — the bringing forth of its water and its pasture and the setting of its mountains — came after the building of the heaven, as the sūrah of those who are dragged forth states: (79:30 — **“As for the earth, He spread it out as well”**).

The verse closes on the knowledge: He has knowledge of all things. The creation of the earth for the human and the building of the seven heavens are the works of the One who knows everything, and the knowledge is the guarantee of the argument: the one who created the creation with knowledge will return to it with knowledge, and nothing of the servant's affair is hidden from the One who made the earth for him and the heaven above him. The verse is the completion of the proof of the previous verse: the one who created the earth and the heaven is the one who gave the two lives, and the two proofs stand together as the ground of the worship that the sūrah will command.

**THE REPORT OF THE BEGINNING**

Al-Suddī gives the verse its report of the beginning, in the wide chain he carries from Abū Mālik and from Abū Ṣāliḥ, from Ibn ʿAbbās, and from Murrah al-Hamdānī, from Ibn Masʿūd, and from people of the Companions: the Throne of God was upon the water, and He had not created anything before the water of what He created; and when He willed to create the creation, He brought out of the water a smoke, and it rose above the water and rose up over it, and He named it sky; then He dried the water and made it one earth, then He split it and made it seven earths in two days — on Sunday and Monday; and He created the earth upon a whale, and the whale is the nūn that God mentioned in the Qurʾān (68:1 — **“Nũn. By the pen and what everyone writes”**), and the whale is in the water, and the water is upon the back of a rock. And al-Rabīʿ ibn Anas read, then He turned to the sky, as, He rose up to the sky.

The verse's for you has its own ruling. Ibn Kaysān said: He created for you — that is, for your sake; and it was said: the meaning is that all that is in the earth is bestowed upon you, so it is for you; and it was said: it is an evidence of the oneness and of the consideration. Al-Qurṭubī states the benefit of it: those who said that the origin of things that are benefited by is permissibility argued with this verse and its like — (45:13 — **“He ˹also˺ subjected for you whatever is in the heavens and whatever is on the earth—all by His grace. Surely in this are signs for people who reflect”**). And of the closing, and He is of all things knowing, al-Ṭabarī says: the knowing is in the meaning of the knower; and it is reported from Ibn ʿAbbās that he used to say: He is the one who is complete in His knowledge. The furniture of the earth is given to the people as a trust of the provision, and the seven heavens are built above them as the roof of the trial — and the Knower of all things is the witness of what they do in the furniture.

---

## Verse 2:30

> ˹Remember˺ when your Lord said to the angels, “I am going to place a successive ˹human˺ authority on earth.” They asked ˹Allah˺, “Will You place in it someone who will spread corruption there and shed blood while we glorify Your praises and proclaim Your holiness?” Allah responded, “I know what you do not know.”

**THE ANNOUNCEMENT TO THE ANGELS**

The argument now passes from the creation of the earth and the heaven to the creation of the one for whom the earth was created, and the sūrah tells the story of the first human. The verse opens with the announcement of the Lord to the angels: I am going to place a khalīfah on the earth — a successor, one generation after another, who will stand in the earth with the authority of the One who placed him. Al-Ḥasan and Qatādah read the announcement as the informing of the deed: He said to them, I am doing it, so the verse is not a consultation but the declaration of the decree to the ones who will witness it. The word khalīfah is the word of the human's standing on the earth, and the sūrah will return to it in the promise to the believers, that He will make them successors in the land as He made those before them successors: (24:55 — **“Allah has promised those of you who believe and do good that He will certainly make them successors in the land, as He did with those before them”**), and in the word given to Dāwūd: (38:26 — **“O David! We have surely made you an authority in the land, so judge between people with truth”**). Al-Qurṭubī stated, with other scholars, that the verse proves the obligation of appointing a khalīfah who passes judgement in the disputes between the people, aids the oppressed against the oppressor, implements the penal code and forbids evil, for these are matters that cannot be fulfilled without an imam, and what is necessary to perform an obligation is itself an obligation.

**THE QUESTION AND THE ANSWER**

The angels answered the announcement with a question: will You place in it someone who will spread corruption there and shed blood, while we glorify Your praises and proclaim Your holiness? The early scholars stated that the question was not a dispute with Allah and not envy of the children of Adam, as some mistakenly thought; it was the question of the ones who seek to know, and their saying while we glorify Your praises was not a commendation of themselves but a description of the standing they were created for. The answer of the verse is short and complete: I know what you do not know. The creation that the angels saw only its corruption and its blood carries in it the prophets, the truthful, the martyrs and the righteous, and the knowledge of Allah encompasses what their knowledge did not reach. The angels are the witnesses of the servants and the carriers of their deeds, and the Ṣaḥīḥ records their standing with the children of Adam: the angels ascend to Allah with the records of the servants' deeds, and He asks them, while having better knowledge, how did you leave My servants, and they say, we came to them while they were praying and left them while they were praying, for the angels work in shifts with mankind and change shifts at the Fajr and the ʿAṣr prayers. The verse is the first word of the human story, and its answer is the standing answer of the verse over every despair of the human: the One who placed the khalīfah knows what the angels did not know.

**THE DIALOGUE IN THE REPORTS**

Al-Suddī gives the dialogue its form, in the wide chain he carries from Abū Mālik and from Abū Ṣāliḥ, from Ibn ʿAbbās, and from Murrah al-Hamdānī, from Ibn Masʿūd, and from people of the Companions: God said to the angels, indeed, I am placing in the earth a khalīfah; they said, our Lord, and what will that khalīfah be? He said: he will have offspring who spread corruption in the earth and envy one another, and some of them will kill some. So the interpretation of the verse, upon this report, is: I am placing in the earth a khalīfah from Me, who succeeds Me in the judgment between My creation. Al-Ṭabarī reads the dialogue against the reading that would fault the angels: if God informed the angels that the offspring of the khalīfah He places in the earth will spread corruption in it and shed blood, and the angels said to their Lord, will You place in it one who spreads corruption in it and sheds blood — there is no face for reproaching them for reporting of one whom God had reported to them that he will spread corruption and shed blood, with the like of what their Lord reported to them of them, so that it could be said to them, of the knowledge that was folded from them, if you were truthful in what you knew by the report of God to you, then report to us what God has folded from you of His knowledge. And Kathīr states the standing answer of the scene: the statement that the angels uttered was not of the disputing with God, nor of the envy of the Children of Ādam, as some mistakenly thought.

Al-Qurṭubī gathers the verse's questions: the when is for the past and the if is for the future, and each may be placed in the place of the other; and al-Naḍr ibn Shumayl said: the malak has no derivation with the Arabs; and the masters of the meanings said: God addressed the angels not for consultation, but for the bringing out of what was in them of the seeing of the movements and the worship and the glorification and the sanctification; then He returned them to their measure, when He said, prostrate to Ādam. The verse is the first council of the human story, and its record is kept in the Book: the question of the angels stands, and the answer of God stands above it — I know what you do not know.

---

## Verse 2:31

> He taught Adam the names of all things, then He presented them to the angels and said, “Tell Me the names of these, if what you say is true?”

**THE KNOWLEDGE OF THE NAMES**

The verse states the gift that made the khalīfah. Allah taught Adam the names of all things, and the early scholars read the names as the whole language of the creation. Ibn ʿAbbās said: the names that people use — human, animal, sky, earth, land, sea, horse, donkey and the names of the other species; and when he was asked whether Allah taught him even the names of the plate and the pot, he said: yes, and even the terms for breaking wind. The teaching is the distinction of the human over the angels: the angels who asked the question of corruption and blood are now shown what they did not know, and what they are shown is not a hidden matter of the heavens but the names of the things of the earth — the knowledge of the one who will be placed in it. The verse then describes the test: Allah presented the things to the angels, and Qatādah said He paraded the objects before them, and said: tell Me the names of these, if what you say is true. The condition returns their question upon them: the ones who asked about the corruption and the blood are asked about the names, and the question they cannot answer is the answer to the question they asked.

The knowledge of the names is the standing honour of the father of mankind, and it is the honour the believers will mention when they seek his intercession on the Day, in what the two Ṣaḥīḥs record: the believers will gather and say, we should seek a means of intercession with our Lord, and they will come to Adam and say, O Adam, you are the father of all mankind, Allah created you with His own hand, ordered the angels to prostrate before you, and taught you the names of everything — will you not intercede for us with your Lord? The verse is thus the first of the sūrah's descriptions of the human's honour, and the honour is knowledge: the khalīfah is the one who was taught, and the teaching is the ground of the vicegerency. The knowledge is a gift received, and the word of the one who receives it is the word the sūrah of the Clot commands the Prophet to say of every knowledge after it: (20:114 — **“and pray, “My Lord! Increase me in knowledge.””**).

**THE CLAY AND THE NAMES**

The reports give the making of Ādam its matter and the teaching its width. Saʿīd ibn Jubayr carries from Ibn ʿAbbās: the Lord of might sent the angel of death, and he took from the surface of the earth, from its sweet and its salt, and He created Ādam from it; and from that he was named Ādam, because he was created from the surface of the earth. And al-Suddī's wide chain said: when the angel of death was sent to take from the earth the clay of Ādam, he took from the face of the earth and mixed, and did not take from one place, and he took of red clay and white and black; and for that the children of Ādam came out differing; and for that he was named Ādam, because he was taken from the surface of the earth.

And the teaching is read in the reports by its width. Al-Ḍaḥḥāk carries from Ibn ʿAbbās: God taught Ādam the names, all of them — and they are these names by which the people know one another: human and beast, and earth and plain, and sea and mountain, and donkey, and the like of those, of the kinds and others. And Mujāhid said: He taught him the name of every thing; and al-Rabīʿ read it: the name of every thing. And Ibn ʿAbbās carries the teaching to its smallest vessels, in the reports of ʿĀṣim ibn Kullayb: He taught him the name of the bowl, and of the small wind and the lesser wind; and in his other report: until al-hānah and al-hunayyah and the wind. The teaching is not the naming of the great alone but the naming of all — and the report of the bowl and the wind stands in the verse as the proof that the all is an all indeed.

---

## Verse 2:32

> They replied, “Glory be to You! We have no knowledge except what You have taught us. You are truly the All-Knowing, All-Wise.”

**THE SUBMISSION OF THE ANGELS**

The verse records the answer of the angels to the test, and it is the answer of the servants: glory be to You. The tasbīḥ is the standing word of the angels when the greatness of the Lord appears to them, and it is the word with which they began their question — while we glorify Your praises — and the word with which they close their answer. Then they confess the limit of their knowledge: we have no knowledge except what You have taught us. The confession is the angels' own statement of the principle the verse has just tested: the knowledge is Allah's gift, and what the angels have of it is what He taught them, and what Adam has of it is what He taught him, and the difference between them is the difference of the teaching. The verse closes on the two names that describe the whole scene: You are the All-Knowing, whose knowledge encompasses what the angels did not know, the All-Wise, whose wisdom placed the khalīfah in the earth and taught him the names, and whose wisdom the question of the angels did not reach.

Al-Bukhārī placed this verse in the Book of Tafsīr of his Ṣaḥīḥ and explained it with the report of Anas ibn Mālik in which the Messenger of Allah ﷺ described the standing of the Day of Resurrection, when the people seek intercession and come to Adam remembering what Allah gave him of the teaching. The verse is the standing lesson of the scene: the angels, with their glorifying and their holiness, did not claim what they were not taught, and the human's knowledge is a received knowledge, taken from the teaching of the One who taught. The submission of the angels is the model the sūrah will hold before the ones it calls to worship: the servant is the one who confesses what he has been given and returns the knowledge to its Giver. The same confession stands in the sūrah's own verse of the Throne, where the knowledge of the angels is described as what He wills to give them: (2:255 — **“He ˹fully˺ knows what is ahead of them and what is behind them, but no one can grasp any of His knowledge—except what He wills ˹to reveal˺”**).

**THE GLORIFICATION AND THE ADMITTING**

Al-Ḍaḥḥāk carries from Ibn ʿAbbās the reading of the angels' word: they said, glory be to You — the declaring of God pure, that any should know the unseen other than Him; we turn to You; we have no knowledge except what You taught us — the declaring of themselves free of the knowledge of the unseen, except what You taught us, as You taught Ādam. And al-Ṭabarī reads the closing: You, You are the Knowing, the Wise — You are, our Lord, the Knower without teaching, of all that has been and all that is to be, and the Knower of the unseen beyond all Your creation — for they negated from themselves, by their saying, we have no knowledge except what You taught us, that they should have any knowledge except what their Lord taught them, and they established what they negated from themselves for their Lord by their saying, You, You are the Knowing — meaning, the Knower without teaching, since what is other than You knows nothing except by the teaching of another; and the Wise is the Possessor of wisdom. And Ibn ʿAbbās said, in the report of ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭalḥah: the Knowing is the one who is complete in His knowledge, and the Wise is the one who is complete in His decree.

Al-Qurṭubī gives the verse its discipline: glory be to You, that is, the declaring of You pure, that any should know the unseen other than You; and this is their answer to His saying, inform Me — and they answered that they do not know except what He informed them of, and they did not reach for what they had no knowledge of, as the ignorant among us do. And al-Ṭabarī gives the scene its place in the sūrah's argument: in these three verses is the consideration for the one who considers, and the reminder for the one who remembers, and the making plain for the one who has a heart — of what God made a proof therein for His Prophet ﷺ upon those who were between his two hands of the Jews of the Children of Israel, by His acquainting him with the sciences of the unseen that God had not acquainted with of His creation except a few, and whose knowledge was not reached except by the telling and the reporting — so that the soundness of his prophethood might be established with them, and they might know that what he brought them with is from Him.

---

## Verse 2:33

> Allah said, “O Adam! Inform them of their names.” Then when Adam did, Allah said, “Did I not tell you that I know the secrets of the heavens and the earth, and I know what you reveal and what you conceal?”

**THE TEST CONCLUDED**

The scene reaches its conclusion. Allah commands Adam to inform the angels of the names, and Adam names them: Ibn ʿAbbās said he named the name of the pigeon, the crow and everything, and statements of similar meaning were reported from Saʿīd ibn Jubayr, al-Ḥasan and Qatādah. When Adam's virtue over the angels became apparent by the names he mentioned, Allah returned to the question the angels had asked, and the return carries the whole argument of the scene: did I not tell you that I know the secrets of the heavens and the earth, and I know what you reveal and what you conceal? The saying of the Lord refers to His word I know what you do not know, and the verse now unfolds it: the knowledge of the unseen of the heavens and the earth is His, and the knowledge of what the servants reveal and what they conceal is His, and the placement of the khalīfah was the decision of that knowledge.

Ibn ʿAbbās said, in the report of al-Ḍaḥḥāk, that what you reveal and what you conceal means: I know the secrets as I know the apparent things, such as what Iblīs concealed in his heart of arrogance and pride; and al-Rabīʿ ibn Anas read the revealing as the apparent part of the affair of the angels and the concealing as what was hidden from it. The verse is thus the first appearance of Iblīs in the sūrah, appearing not by name but by his concealed state, one verse before the sūrah names him in the refusal of the prostration. The scene closes with the knowledge of Allah established over the whole affair: the question of the angels, the teaching of Adam, and the arrogance that was still concealed — and the servant reads the scene knowing that his own revealing and concealing stand before the same knowledge. The Book states the same knowledge in the sūrah of Ṭā Hā: (20:7 — **“Whether you speak openly ˹or not˺, He certainly knows what is secret and what is even more hidden”**).

**WHAT THEY MADE MANIFEST AND WHAT THEY HID**

The reports divide the two words of the verse. Al-Ḍaḥḥāk carries from Ibn ʿAbbās: He said, O Ādam, inform them of their names — that is, tell them their names; and when he informed them of their names, He said, did I not say to you, O angels, in particular: indeed I know the unseen of the heavens and the earth, and none other than Me knows it? And of His saying, and I know what you make manifest and what you were hiding, Ibn ʿAbbās said, in the same chain: I know what you make manifest, he says, what you show, and what you were hiding, he says, I know the secret as I know the open — meaning, what Iblīs concealed in himself of pride and deception. And al-Suddī's wide chain said: their saying, will You place in it one who spreads corruption in it — that is what they made manifest; and what you were hiding means what Iblīs kept secret in himself of pride. And al-Rabīʿ ibn Anas read it: what they made manifest was when they said, will You place in it one who spreads corruption in it; and what they hid among themselves was their saying, our Lord will not create a creation but that we are more knowing than it and more noble; and they knew that God had preferred Ādam over them in knowledge and in honour.

Al-Ṭabarī answers the grammar of the hiding: if one supposes that the report of the hiding of the angels, since it comes out in the form of a report about all of them, cannot be a report about the hiding of Iblīs — he has supposed other than the right, for it is of the way of the Arabs, when they report a report about some of a group without naming the person in particular, to bring the report out in the form of a report about all of them, as in their saying, the army was killed and they were routed, when only one or some were killed and routed. And al-Qurṭubī states the rank that the scene established: He commanded him to inform them of their names after He presented them to the angels, so that they might know that he was more knowing of what He asked them about — a calling of attention to his excellence and the height of his standing; so he was more excellent than them in that He placed him before them and made them prostrate to him and made them his students and commanded them to learn from him; and there came to him the rank of majesty and greatness, in that He made him the one prostrated to and singled him out with knowledge.

---

## Verse 2:34

> And ˹remember˺ when We said to the angels, “Prostrate before Adam,” so they all did—but not Iblîs, who refused and acted arrogantly, becoming unfaithful

**THE PROSTRATION AND THE REFUSAL**

The scene passes from the test of the names to the honour of the one who was tested, and the verse commands the angels: prostrate before Adam. The prostration was before Adam, but the obedience was to Allah: the angels prostrated in obedience to the command, and Adam was the direction of the honour, not the one worshipped. The verse records the compliance of all of them and then excepts one: but not Iblīs. The exception is followed by his three verbs: he refused, and he was arrogant, and he was of the disbelievers. The refusal is the act of the will, the arrogance is the state of the heart behind it, and the disbelief is the standing the two earned him; the verse gathers the three in order, because the arrogance is the root, the refusal is its fruit, and the disbelief is their harvest.

Iblīs was not one of the angels in kind, although he was included in the command addressed to them. The sūrah of the Cave states his origin: (18:50 — **“but not Iblîs, who was one of the jinn, but he rebelled against the command of his Lord”**), and Muhammad ibn Isḥāq reported from Ibn ʿAbbās that before he undertook the path of sin, Iblīs was with the angels and was called ʿAzāzīl; he was among the residents of the earth and was one of the most active worshippers and knowledgeable of the angels, and that caused him to be arrogant, and he was from a genus called Jinn. The verse is the sūrah's first lesson in the danger of worship without submission: the one who had worshipped with the angels fell by the first command that crossed his own will, and the arrogance that had been concealed in the previous verse now appeared in the refusal.

**WHAT IBLĪS WAS AND WHAT THE REFUSAL MEANS**

The reports give Iblīs his genealogy. Al-Ḍaḥḥāk carries from Ibn ʿAbbās: Iblīs was of a tribe of the tribes of the angels who are called the Jinn, created from the fire of the samūm, from among the angels; and his name was al-Ḥārith; and he was one of the treasurers of Paradise; and the angels were created of a light other than this tribe; and the jinn who are mentioned in the Qurʾān were created from the tongue of the fire, which is the flame that is at its edge when it blazes. And Ibn ʿAbbās said, in the report of ʿIkrimah: God created a creation and said, prostrate to Ādam; and they said, we will not do it; so God sent upon them a fire that burned them; then He created another creation and said, I am creating a human of clay — prostrate to Ādam; and they refused, so God sent upon them a fire and it burned them; then He created these, and said, prostrate to Ādam, and they said, yes — and Iblīs was of those who refused to prostrate to Ādam.

Al-Ṭabarī reads the refusal as the standing mirror of the sūrah: this, though it is a report from God about Iblīs, is a rebuke of his likes among the creation of God, who are arrogant from humbling themselves to the command of God and from yielding to His obedience; and among those who were arrogant were the Jews who were between the two sides of the place of the emigration of the Messenger of God ﷺ, and their scholars, who knew the Messenger of God ﷺ and his description and knew that he was a messenger of God; then they were arrogant, with their knowledge; and He described Iblīs with the like of what He described them with, of pride and envy and disdain, when He said, and he was of the disbelievers — of the ones who deny the favors of God upon him, in his opposing Him in what He commanded of the prostration to Ādam, as the Jews denied the favors of their Lord that He gave them and their fathers before them, of the feeding of their forefathers with the manna and the quails and the shading of the cloud. And al-Qurṭubī carries the debate of the prostration's direction: those who preferred Ādam and his sons argued with His saying, prostrate to Ādam, that it indicates he was more excellent than them; and the answer is that the meaning is, prostrate to Me, facing Ādam — as in His saying (17:78 — **“Observe the prayer from the decline of the sun until the darkness of the night”**), and His saying (38:72 — **“So when I have fashioned him and had a spirit of My Own ˹creation˺ breathed into him, fall down in prostration to him.””**).

---

## Verse 2:35
