> We cautioned, “O Adam! Live with your wife in Paradise and eat as freely as you please, but do not approach this tree, or else you will be wrongdoers.”

**THE DWELLING AND THE ONE PROHIBITION**

The honour of the prostration is complete, and the verse gives Adam his place. Allah honoured Adam by commanding the angels to prostrate before him, and then He allowed him to live and to eat wherever and whatever he wished in Paradise: live with your wife in the garden, and eat freely from wherever you will. The gift is wide and the condition is one: do not approach this tree. The prohibition is the first test of the khalīfah, and its form is the form of the tests of the sūrah: the permission is broad, the prohibition is single, and the warning names the consequence — or you will be of the wrongdoers, the ones who have wronged themselves by crossing the one boundary in the middle of the garden. The verse does not name the tree, and the knowledge of which tree it was is not part of what the servant is commanded; the test is in the boundary itself, not in the tree behind it. The same dwelling and the same prohibition stand in the sūrah of the Heights: (7:19 — **“O Adam! Live with your wife in Paradise and eat from wherever you please, but do not approach this tree, or else you will be wrongdoers.”**).

The reports honour Adam before and after the dwelling. Abū Dharr asked the Messenger of Allah ﷺ: was Adam a prophet? and he said: yes, a prophet and a messenger to whom Allah spoke directly. And Ibn ʿAbbās said, in what al-Ḥākim recorded, that Adam was allowed to reside in Paradise during the time between the ʿAṣr prayer and sunset — a report that states how short the dwelling was before the test came to it. The wife is addressed with Adam in the dwelling and in the eating and in the warning, and the verse thus establishes the first house of the human race in the garden: the two are together in the gift and together in the prohibition, and the sūrah will carry the story of the two through the fall and the repentance and the descent.

**THE MAKING OF EVE AND THE TREE**

Al-Ṭabarī draws the order of the story from the verse itself: in this verse is a clear indication of the soundness of the saying of the one who said that Iblīs was brought out of the Garden after his arrogance from prostrating to Ādam, and that Ādam was made to dwell in it before Iblīs descended to the earth — for God said, and We said, O Ādam, dwell, you and your wife, in the Garden, and eat of it freely where you will, and do not approach this tree — and then said, and the Devil made them slip from it and brought them out of what they were in; so it is plain that Iblīs made them slip from the obedience of God only after he was cursed and showed his arrogance, for the prostration of the angels to Ādam was after the spirit was blown into him. And al-Suddī's wide chain said: the enemy of God Iblīs swore by the might of God to mislead Ādam and his offspring and his wife — except His sincere servants among them — after God cursed him, and after he was brought out of the Garden, and before he descended to the earth.

And the making of the wife is carried in the same chain: Iblīs was brought out of the Garden when he was cursed, and Ādam was made to dwell in the Garden; and he walked in it alone, with no wife to whom he might be still; and he slept a sleep and woke, and at his head was a woman sitting, whom God had created from his rib; and he asked her, who are you? And she said, a woman. He said, and for what were you created? She said, that you might be still to me. And the angels said to him — looking at what his knowledge had reached — what is her name, O Ādam? He said, Eve. They said, and why was she named Eve? He said, because she was created from a living thing. And God said to him, O Ādam, dwell, you and your wife, in the Garden. The freely is read in the reports: al-Suddī's wide chain said, the free is the pleasant; and Mujāhid said: there is no reckoning upon them; and al-Ḍaḥḥāk, from Ibn ʿAbbās: the free is the width of the provision. And of the tree, Ibn ʿAbbās said, in the report of ʿIkrimah: the tree from the eating of whose fruit Ādam was forbidden is the wheat.

---

## Verse 2:36

> But Satan deceived them—leading to their fall from the ˹blissful˺ state they were in, and We said, “Descend from the heavens ˹to the earth˺ as enemies to each other. You will find in the earth a residence and provision for your appointed stay.”

**THE SLIP AND THE DESCENT**

The verse records the end of the dwelling: Satan caused them to slip. The early scholars read the slipping in two ways: al-Ḥasan and Qatādah said he tripped them — he made them slip from the tree into the eating of it; and it is read that he removed them from the Paradise itself, so that the slipping was the falling from the state they were in. The verse names the result: he led them out of that in which they were, and the state is not named except by its greatness — the dwelling, the ease, the nearness — so that the loss is measured by what it was. The first enmity of the human story is now declared: the one who refused the prostration to the father became the enemy of the father and his wife, and the descent is pronounced with the enmity standing in it: descend, some of you as enemies to some. The enmity is between the children of Adam and the devil who caused their father to slip, and it is the standing enmity the Book will return to again and again, warning the children of the father from the enemy of the father.

The descent is not the end of the provision: on the earth you will have a dwelling and provision for an appointed time. The earth that was created for the human before he was placed in it now receives him, and the provision is described as his until the term — the term of his life, and the term of the world. The verse closes the first scene of the human story on the earth: the khalīfah has arrived in the place of his vicegerency, and he arrives carrying his enemy with him, and carrying the promise of the guidance that the next verse will state. The same descent and the same enmity stand in the sūrah of Ṭā Hā, where the two are sent down together: (20:121 — **“So Adam disobeyed his Lord, and ˹so˺ lost his way”**), and the descent there is pronounced over the enmity of the two, as it is pronounced here.

**THE SLIP AND ITS MEANS**

The readers read the verb of the slip in two ways, and the verse is weighed by them. The generality read it, the Devil made them slip, with the doubling — meaning, he caused them to slip, of your saying, the man slipped in his religion, when he wavered in it and erred; and God attributed to Iblīs the bringing out of Ādam and his wife from the Garden, since he was the one who caused the mistake for which God punished them by the bringing out. And the worthiest of the two readings is this one, since God informed in the word that follows it that Iblīs brought them out of what they were in, and that is the meaning of the removal. And Ḥamza read it, the Devil removed them, from the removal; and Ibn Kaysān read it of the zawāl — that is, he turned them from what they were upon, of the obedience, to the disobedience.

Al-Suddī gives the slip its means, in the wide chain: when God said to Ādam, dwell, you and your wife, in the Garden, and eat of it freely where you will, and do not approach this tree — Iblīs wished to enter upon the two of them into the Garden, and the treasurers prevented him; so he came to the serpent — and it was a beast with four legs, as if it were a camel, and it was of the most beautiful of beasts — and he spoke to it to bring him in its mouth, until it brought him in to Ādam; and it brought him in its jaw; and the serpent passed by the treasurers and entered, and they did not know, for what God willed of the affair; and he spoke to Ādam from its jaw. Al-Qurṭubī reads the bringing out with the purpose of the enemy: the bringing out of the two of them was from the Garden to the earth, because they were created from it, and so that Ādam would be a khalīfah in the earth; and Iblīs did not intend the bringing out of him from it — he intended only the fall of him from his rank and the distancing of him, as he himself had been distanced — and he did not reach his purpose. The enemy aimed at the fall, and the decree took the descent and made it the beginning of the khilāfah.

---

## Verse 2:37

> Then Adam was inspired with words ˹of prayer˺ by his Lord, so He accepted his repentance. Surely He is the Accepter of Repentance, Most Merciful

**THE WORDS OF THE REPENTANCE**

The verse turns the fall into the beginning of the return. Adam received words from his Lord, and the receiving is from both sides: the Lord gave the words and the servant received them, and the repentance was built on the two. The early scholars read the words as the words the two said when they had wronged themselves, which the sūrah of the Heights records: (7:23 — **“They replied, “Our Lord! We have wronged ourselves. If You do not forgive us and have mercy on us, we will certainly be losers.””**), and this was stated by Mujāhid, Saʿīd ibn Jubayr, Abū al-ʿĀliyah, al-Rabīʿ ibn Anas, al-Ḥasan, Qatādah, and others. In the report of Ibn ʿAbbās, the words were the dialogue Adam held with his Lord: he said, O my Lord, did You not create me with Your own hand? He said, yes. He said, and did You not breathe into me of Your spirit? He said, yes. He said, and when I sneezed, You said, may Allah have mercy on you — does not Your mercy precede Your anger? He was told, yes. He said, and You had destined me to commit this act? He was told, yes. He said, then if I repent, will You return me to Paradise? He said, yes.

The verse then states the acceptance: and his Lord turned to him in mercy — and it closes on the two names that carry the whole story of the repentance: He is the Accepter of repentance, the Most Merciful. The verse is the first repentance in the Book, and its form is the standing form of every repentance after it: the servant wrongs himself, the Lord gives the words, the servant speaks them, and the Lord accepts. The human story in the sūrah began with the khalīfah who was taught, and it is established here that the khalīfah is also the one who repents: the two honours stand together, and the second of them is the one the children of Adam will need most.

**THE WORDS IN THE REPORTS**

The reports carry the words themselves. Saʿīd ibn Jubayr carries from Ibn ʿAbbās: Ādam received from his Lord words, and He turned to him — he said: my Lord, did You not create me with Your hand? He said, yes. He said, my Lord, did You not blow into me of Your spirit? He said, yes. He said, my Lord, did You not make me dwell in Your Garden? He said, yes. He said, my Lord, did not Your mercy precede Your anger? He said, yes. He said, do You see, if I repent and amend, will You return me to the Garden? He said, yes. And Abū al-ʿĀliyah said, in the report of al-Rabīʿ: when Ādam committed the mistake he said, my Lord, do You see, if I repent and amend? And God said, then I return you to the Garden — so that is of the words; and of the words also is (7:23 — **“They replied, “Our Lord! We have wronged ourselves. If You do not forgive us and have mercy on us, we will certainly be losers.””**). And Mujāhid carried the words in their full form: O God, there is no god but You, glory be to You and with Your praise — my Lord, I have wronged myself, so forgive me, for You are the best of forgivers; O God, there is no god but You, glory be to You and with Your praise — my Lord, I have wronged myself, so have mercy on me, for You are the best of those who show mercy; O God, there is no god but You, glory be to You and with Your praise — my Lord, I have wronged myself, so turn to me, for You are the Turner-in-mercy, the Merciful.

Al-Ṭabarī states the reading of the verse: some read it, the words received Ādam, making the words the receiving party — and that, though it is permissible in the face of the Arabic, since all that the man receives is receiving him, and what meets him has met him — yet it is not permissible in the recitation except the raising of Ādam, as the receiver of the words, for the agreement of the proof of the readers and the people of interpretation of the scholars of the early and the late, upon the directing of the receiving to Ādam rather than to the words. And these sayings that we have reported, though they differ in their wordings, agree in their meanings, that God caused Ādam to receive words, and Ādam received them from his Lord and accepted them and acted upon them, and repented by his saying them and his acting upon them.

---

## Verse 2:38

> We said, “Descend all of you! Then when guidance comes to you from Me, whoever follows it, there will be no fear for them, nor will they grieve

**THE COVENANT OF THE GUIDANCE**

The descent is commanded a second time, and with it the covenant that will govern the life of the children of Adam on the earth: when guidance comes to you from Me. Abū al-ʿĀliyah said that the guidance here refers to the prophets, the messengers, the clear signs and the plain explanation, so the covenant is not a word left without bearers: the guidance will come carrying its carriers, and the descent of Adam is followed by the descent of the guidance. The condition of the covenant is the following: whoever follows My guidance, and the following is the standing word of the sūrah for the acceptance of the revelation — the word that joins the knowledge of the Book to the walking by it.

The reward of the covenant is stated in the two negations: there will be no fear upon them, nor will they grieve. The fear is of what is ahead, and the grief is of what is behind, and the two negations together gather the servant's time — he neither dreads what is coming nor sorrows over what is gone. The same covenant was given at the descent of the two, in the sūrah of Ṭā Hā: (20:123 — **“Then when guidance comes to you from Me, whoever follows My guidance will neither go astray ˹in this life˺ nor suffer ˹in the next˺”**), and the two forms of the covenant stand together: the following of the guidance is the safety from straying and from grief, in this world and in the next. The verse is the bridge between the story of Adam and the story of his children: the sūrah has established the father, his honour, his slip, his repentance and his descent, and now it addresses the children with the covenant that descended with him.

**THE GUIDANCE THAT FOLLOWS THE DESCENT**

The reports read the guidance by its bearers. Abū al-ʿĀliyah said: then if there comes to you from Me guidance — the guidance is the prophets and the messengers and the making plain; and he read, then whoever follows My guidance, as, My making plain. Al-Ṭabarī works the verse's address through: if what Abū al-ʿĀliyah said is as he said it, then the address of go down, though it is to Ādam and his wife, must intend Ādam and his wife and their offspring together — for Ādam was the prophet in the days of his life after he was brought down to the earth, and the messenger from God to his children, and it is not possible that he, being the messenger, is intended by the saying, then if there comes to you from Me guidance, addressed to him and his wife, as, then if there come to you prophets and messengers, except upon that interpretation. And the saying nearer to the right in al-Ṭabarī's sight is: then if there comes to you, O company of those brought down to the earth from My heaven — Ādam and his wife and Iblīs — if there comes to you from Me the making plain of My command and My obedience, and the guidance to My path and My religion; and though it is an address to those who were brought down then, it is the way of God with all His creation, and the making known thereby of those of whom He told at the opening of this sūrah, that His judgement is upon them as it is upon those who were brought down.

Al-Qurṭubī reads the doubled command: We said, go down — He repeated the command in the way of the intensification and its confirmation, as you say to a man, stand, stand; and it was said that He repeated it because He attached to each of the two commands a ruling other than the ruling of the other — to the first the enmity, and to the second the coming of the guidance; and it was said that the first descent is from the Garden to the heaven, and the second from the heaven to the earth — and upon this there is in it an indication that the Garden is in the seventh heaven, as the ḥadīth of the Night Journey indicates. And Wahb ibn Munabbih carries the first hour upon the earth: when Ādam came down to the earth, Iblīs said to the beasts, this is your enemy, so destroy him; and they gathered and entrusted their affair to the dog, and said, you are the bravest of us, and made him a chief; and when Ādam saw that, he was bewildered; and Jibrīl came to him and said, pass your hand over the head of the dog; and he did so; and when the beasts saw that the dog had grown familiar with Ādam, they dispersed; and the dog sought his security, and Ādam gave him security, and he remained with him and with his children.

---

## Verse 2:39

> But those who disbelieve and deny Our signs will be the residents of the Fire. They will be there forever.”

**THE OTHER END OF THE COVENANT**

The covenant states its other end. The two negations of the verse before — no fear and no grief — are the portion of the ones who follow the guidance, and the verse gives the portion of the ones who disbelieve and belie the signs: those are the companions of the Fire, and they will abide in it forever. The two descriptions are the two halves of the rejection: the disbelief of the heart and the belying of the tongue, and the signs they belie are the signs the guidance came carrying. The word companions, aṣḥāb, is the word of the companionship and the sticking: the ones of the Fire are named its companions because they stick to it as the companion sticks to his companion, and the abiding is forever.

The verse closes the story of Adam on its two ends, and the two ends are the standing two ends of the whole Book: the following of the guidance and the safety, or the disbelief and the Fire. Ibn ʿAbbās read the promise that stands beside this threat — the saying of the sūrah of Ṭā Hā, whoever follows My guidance will neither go astray nor be wretched — as meaning that he will not be misguided in this life or miserable in the Hereafter; and the same Book states the other side of the pair, the turning away that this verse describes: (20:124 — **“But whoever turns away from My Reminder will certainly have a miserable life, then We will raise them up blind on the Day of Judgment.”**). The same end of the deniers stands in the sūrah of the Table, in the identical words: (5:10 — **“As for those who disbelieve and deny Our signs, they are the residents of the Hellfire”**).

The verse is the last word of the first address of the sūrah, the address of the human as human, and the sūrah will now turn to the house that received the guidance before this Book: the children of Israel.

**THE PEOPLE OF THE FIRE AND THE PEOPLE OF THE SINS**

Al-Ṭabarī reads the verse's words: and those who disbelieve — that is, deny My signs and deny My messengers; and the signs of God are His proofs and His evidences upon His oneness and His lordship, and what the messengers brought of the tokens and the witnesses upon that, and upon their truth in what they reported of their Lord; and We have made plain that the meaning of the disbelief is the covering of the thing. And those are the companions of the fire — its people, who are its people other than others, the ones made to abide in it forever, without term and without end. And the Messenger of God ﷺ gives the abiding its limit and its exception, in what Abū Saʿīd al-Khudrī carries: as for the people of the fire who are its people, they will not die in it and will not live; but people whom the fire overtakes by their offences or by their sins, He will cause them to die a death, until when they have become charcoal, permission will be given for the intercession.

Al-Qurṭubī gives the companionship its language: and those who disbelieve, that is, associate others — because of His saying, and deny Our signs; and the ṣuḥbah is the being joined to the thing in some state, in some time; and if there is constant attendance and mixing, that is the perfection of the companionship — and such is the companionship of the people of the fire with it. The verse closes the opening arc of the sūrah: the guidance has come down, and the two endings stand named — no fear upon the followers of the guidance, and no grief, and the companionship of the fire for the ones who deny — and between the two endings stands all of the history the sūrah will now tell.

---

## Verse 2:40

> O children of Israel! Remember My favours upon you. Fulfil your covenant and I will fulfil Mine, and stand in awe of Me ˹alone˺

**THE FIRST ADDRESS TO THE CHILDREN OF ISRAEL**

The sūrah turns from the story of the father of mankind to the story of the house that carried the revelation before this Book, and the address opens with the name of the house: O children of Israel. Israel is Yaʿqūb, the son of Isḥāq, the son of Ibrāhīm, and the children of Israel are the house of the prophets — the house that the sūrah of al-ʿImrān will describe as the chosen of the worlds in its time. The address begins with the command to remember the favours: remember My favours which I bestowed upon you, and the favours are the favours of the house — the deliverance from Pharaoh, the parting of the sea, the shading of the cloud, the sending of the prophets, the giving of the Book — and the remembering is the remembering that produces the gratitude, and the gratitude is the obedience.

**THE COVENANT AND ITS FULFILMENT**

The verse then states the covenant: fulfil My covenant and I will fulfil your covenant. Ibn ʿAbbās said: My covenant is the covenant I took from you concerning the Prophet Muhammad ﷺ — that when he is sent, you will believe in him and follow him, and I will grant you what I promised you, and I will remove the chains and the restrictions that were placed around your necks because of the errors you committed. Al-Ḥasan al-Baṣrī read the covenant as the covenant Allah took from the children of Israel with the twelve leaders, which the sūrah of the Table records: (5:12 — **“Allah made a covenant with the Children of Israel and appointed twelve leaders from among them”**), and the covenant of the prophets, which the sūrah of Āl ʿImrān records: (3:81 — **“˹Remember˺ when Allah made a covenant with the prophets, ˹saying,˺ “Now that I have given you the Book and wisdom, if there comes to you a messenger confirming what you have, you must believe in him and support him.””**). The covenant of the verse is thus the covenant the whole house stands under, and the fulfilment of Allah is promised upon the fulfilment of the servant. The verse closes on the fear of the One: and fear Me alone — the same fear with which the first address of the sūrah opened, and the fear is the standing condition of every covenant in the Book.

**THE NAME, THE FAVOR, AND THE ADDRESSEES**

The reports give the address its name and its people. Ibn ʿAbbās said, in the report of al-Aʿmash: Isrāʾīl is like your saying ʿAbd Allāh — the servant of God. And al-Ṭabarī fixes the addressees: God addressed by His saying, O Children of Isrāʾīl, the scholars of the Jews of the Children of Israel, who were between the two sides of the place of the emigration of the Messenger of God ﷺ; and He ascribed them to Yaʿqūb as He ascribed the offspring of Ādam to Ādam, when He said (7:31 — **“O Children of Adam! Dress properly whenever you are at worship. Eat and drink, but do not waste”**); and He singled them out with the address in this verse and in the verses after it, in which He reminded them of His favors, because the proofs and the signs He argued with contain the reports of their forefathers and the news of their ancients, and the stories of the matters whose knowledge is their special portion rather than other nations — and there is not with other than them of the knowledge of its soundness and its reality the like of what is with them. And Ibn Isḥāq carries from Ibn ʿAbbās: O Children of Isrāʾīl — that is, O People of the Book, to the scholars of the Jews.

And the favor is read in the reports by its faces. Ibn Isḥāq carries from Ibn ʿAbbās: remember My favor, which I bestowed upon you — that is, My graces upon you and upon your fathers, for what He saved them by, from Pharaoh and his people. And Abū al-ʿĀliyah said: His favor is that He made from among them the prophets and the messengers, and sent down upon them the Books. And Mujāhid said: remember My favor, which I bestowed upon you — meaning, His favor upon the Children of Israel, in what He named and in what is other than that: He split for them the stone, and sent down upon them the manna and the quails, and saved them from the servitude of the people of Pharaoh. And Ibn Isḥāq carries the covenant: and fulfill My covenant — which I took in the Torah upon you. The favor comes first in the verse because the covenant stands upon it: the remembering of the favor is the ground of the fulfilling, and the fulfilling is the condition of the fearing.

---

## Verse 2:41

> Believe in My revelations which confirm your Scriptures. Do not be the first to deny them or trade them for a fleeting gain. And be mindful of Me

**THE BOOK THAT CONFIRMS THEIRS**

The covenant of the previous verse now takes its first command: believe in what I have sent down, confirming what is with you. The one sent down is the Qurʾān, which Allah sent down to Muhammad ﷺ, the unlettered Arab Prophet, as a bringer of good news, a warner and a light, and it contains the truth from Allah and confirms what was revealed before it in the Torah and the Gospel. The confirming is the hinge of the address: the Book they are called to is not a rival of their Book but its witness, and their own Scripture is the one that described it before it came. The Book states the same standing of this Prophet in the sūrah of the Heights: (7:157 — **“the unlettered Prophet, whose description they find in their Torah and the Gospel”**).

The verse then gives the two prohibitions that guard the belief. The first: do not be the first to disbelieve in it. Ibn ʿAbbās said: do not become the first to disbelieve in the Qurʾān while you have more knowledge of it than other people; and Abū al-ʿĀliyah said: do not become the first, from among the People of the Book, to disbelieve in Muhammad after you hear that he was sent as a Prophet, and the like was said by al-Ḥasan, al-Suddī and al-Rabīʿ ibn Anas. The weight of the prohibition is in the first: the one who knew the signs first is the last one permitted to deny them first, and the priority in knowledge becomes, if betrayed, a priority in the offence. The second prohibition: do not exchange My signs for a little price — the standing of the truth is not to be sold for the standing of this world, and the sūrah of the Family of ʿImrān will praise the ones of the People of the Book who refused that trade: (3:199 — **“never trading Allah’s revelations for a fleeting gain”**). The verse closes on the fear of the One: and fear Me alone — the same closing as the covenant verse, because the whole affair of the address is held between the remembering of the favours and the fear of the Favoured.

**THE CONFIRMING AND THE FIRST DENIER**

Al-Ṭabarī reads the verse clause by clause: believe — affirm, as has been made plain before; and in what I have sent down, what was sent down upon Muḥammad ﷺ of the Qurʾān; and confirming what is with you — that the Qurʾān confirms what is with the Jews of the Children of Israel, of the Torah; so He commanded them the affirmation of the Qurʾān, and informed them that in their affirmation of the Qurʾān is their affirmation of the Torah, because what is in the Qurʾān of the command to acknowledge the prophethood of Muḥammad ﷺ and to affirm him and to follow him is the equal of what is of that in the Torah and the Gospel — so in their affirmation of what was sent down upon Muḥammad is their affirmation of what is with them of the Torah, and in their denial of it is their denial of what is with them of the Torah. And Mujāhid read it: I sent down the Qurʾān only confirming what is with you, of the Torah and the Gospel; and Abū al-ʿĀliyah read it: O People of the Book, believe in what I sent down upon Muḥammad, confirming what is with you — because they find Muḥammad ﷺ written with them in the Torah and the Gospel.

And of the first denier, al-Ṭabarī settles the reference: God commanded the addressed in the first of the verse to believe in what He sent down upon Muḥammad ﷺ, and it is of the known that what God sent down in the age of Muḥammad ﷺ is the Qurʾān, not Muḥammad, for Muḥammad is a messenger sent, not a revelation sent down, and what is sent down is the Book; then He forbade them to be the first to disbelieve in what He commanded them to believe in at the opening of the verse — and Muḥammad ﷺ had no explicit mention in this verse upon which the hidden pronoun of and do not be the first to disbelieve in it could return. And Ibn Isḥāq carries from Ibn ʿAbbās: and do not be the first to disbelieve in it, while with you of the knowledge of it is what is not with others. The verse binds the two Books together: the affirmation of the second is the affirmation of the first, and the denial of the second is the denial of the first — and the knowledge they carry is the argument against being the first.

---

## Verse 2:42

> Do not mix truth with falsehood or hide the truth knowingly

**THE TWO CORRUPTIONS OF THE TRUTH**

The verse forbids the two acts by which the truth is corrupted in the hand of the one who carries it. The first is the mixing: do not clothe the truth with falsehood. Ibn ʿAbbās said, in the report of al-Ḍaḥḥāk: do not mix the truth with falsehood, and the facts with lies; and Qatādah read it of the religions: do not mix Judaism and Christianity with Islam, while you know that the religion of Allah is Islam, and that Judaism and Christianity are innovations that did not come from Allah. The mixing is more dangerous than the replacing, because the replaced truth is seen to be gone, while the mixed truth still wears the appearance of the truth. The second is the concealing: do not hide the truth while you know. The two prohibitions are the two forms of the one offence: the one who mixes corrupts the truth that he gives, and the one who hides withholds the truth that he owes, and the verse adds the word knowingly to state that the offence is measured by the knowledge of its doer.

The verse is followed by the command of its opposite, which the scholars drew from it: Allah forbade them from the two acts and ordered them to make the truth known and to explain it. The verse is thus the standing law of the scholars of every Book: the truth is not to be mixed, and it is not to be hidden, and the knowledge of it is the obligation of its declaration. The same reproach returns upon the house in the sūrah of the Family of ʿImrān, in the identical words: (3:71 — **“O People of the Book! Why do you mix the truth with falsehood and hide the truth knowingly”**). The sūrah will return to the concealing of the truth in the later verses of this address, and it will name its doers and its price, and state that the one who hides what Allah sent down eats fire into his belly.

**WHAT THEY CONCEALED AND KNEW**

The reports name the concealed truth. Al-Ḍaḥḥāk carries from Ibn ʿAbbās: and do not conceal the truth while you know — that is, do not conceal the truth while you know; and in the report of Ibn Isḥāq from Ibn ʿAbbās, through ʿIkrimah or Saʿīd ibn Jubayr: and do not conceal the truth. And the concealment is given its object: Abū al-ʿĀliyah said, they concealed the sending of Muḥammad ﷺ; and Mujāhid said the like. And Ibn Isḥāq carries from Ibn ʿAbbās: and do not conceal the truth — do not conceal what is with you of the knowledge of My Messenger and of what he brought, while you find it with you in the Books you know that are in your hands. And al-Ḍaḥḥāk carries from Ibn ʿAbbās the second clause: you know that Muḥammad is the Messenger of God — so He forbade them from that. And Mujāhid read the verse whole: the People of the Book concealed Muḥammad ﷺ, while they find him written with them in the Torah and the Gospel. The concealment of the verse is not the concealment of a doubtful thing but the concealment of a found thing — written in the Books, known in the hands — and the while you know stands over it as the witness that removes every excuse.

---

## Verse 2:43

> Establish prayer, pay alms-tax, and bow down with those who bow down

**THE COMMAND TO JOIN THE COMMUNITY**

The address now passes from the belief and the truth to the acts, and it commands the two acts that gather the religion: establish the prayer and give the zakāh. The prayer is the standing of the servant before his Lord, and the zakāh is the standing of the servant with his brothers, and the two are paired in the Book wherever the religion is gathered into its acts. Muqātil read the three commands of the verse as the three gates of the one entrance: the saying of Allah to the People of the Book, establish the prayer, commands them to perform the prayer behind the Prophet ﷺ, and give the zakāh commands them to pay the zakāh to the Prophet ﷺ, and bow down with those who bow down commands them to bow with those who bow among the nation of Muhammad ﷺ — so Allah commands the People of the Book to be with the nation of Muhammad ﷺ and among it.

The closing of the verse is its deepest word: bow down with those who bow down. The rukūʿ is named as the act of the whole body in the prayer, and the verse does not say bow down to your Lord but bow down with those who bow, because the command is the command of the congregation: the prayer that is established is the prayer with the bowers, and the religion the verse calls the People of the Book to is not a religion of the single soul but of the community that bows together. The verse is the first command of the acts in the sūrah, and it establishes the form that the sūrah will hold throughout: the belief is completed by the acts, and the acts are completed by being with the believers. The same pairing of the prayer and the zakāh stands in the sūrah of the Light, joined there to the obedience of the Messenger: (24:56 — **“Moreover, establish prayer, pay alms-tax, and obey the Messenger, so you may be shown mercy”**).

**THE TWO OBLIGATIONS AND THE BOWING**

Al-Ṭabarī gives the command its occasion: it was mentioned that the scholars of the Jews and the hypocrites used to command the people with the establishing of the prayer and the giving of the zakāh, while they did not do it themselves; so God commanded them the establishing of the prayer with the Muslims who affirm Muḥammad and what he brought, and the giving of the zakāh of their wealth with them, and that they humble themselves to God and His Messenger as they humbled themselves. And Qatādah read it: and establish the prayer and give the zakāh — two obligations made necessary; so give them both to God.

The word of the zakāh carries its own field: the giving of the zakāh is the paying of the obligatory alms; and the root of the zakāh is the growth of the wealth and its fructifying and its increase — and from that it is said, the crop was pure, when what God brought out of it was much, and the spending was pure, when it was much; and it is said, the single was pure, when it became a pair by the increase of the one added to it, until it became an even number by it — as the poet said, they were odd or even, of less than four, not created, while the fortunes of the people struggle; and another said, its number is neither odd nor even, as the worst of the herbs are the tips of the awns; and the awns are the thorns of the grass, which is what is round in the thorn-bearing plant. And the bowing carries its grammar: and bow with those who bow — pray with those who pray, of Muḥammad ﷺ and his Companions; and it is mentioned with the wording of the bowing because it is a pillar of the pillars of the prayer, and because the prayer of the Jews had no bowing in it — so it is as if He said, pray as the prayer of those who bow.

---

## Verse 2:44

> Do you preach righteousness and fail to practice it yourselves, although you read the Scripture? Do you not understand

**THE PREACHING AND THE PRACTICE**

The verse turns on the carriers of the Scripture with the question that exposes them: do you command the people with the righteousness and forget yourselves, while you recite the Scripture? The word of the command is al-birr, the whole of the righteousness — the word the sūrah will later gather into the verse of al-birr — and the verse describes the ones who command it and leave it. The report states their case: the Children of Israel used to command the people to obey Allah, to fear Him and to perform al-birr, and they contradicted their own command, so Allah reminded them of it. Ibn Jurayj said the verse is about the People of the Book and the hypocrites: they used to command the people to pray and to fast, and they did not practice what they commanded; and he drew from it the standing rule: whoever commands the people with righteousness, let him be the first of them to perform it. Ibn ʿAbbās, in the report of Ibn Isḥāq, read the verse of the house's own offence: you forbid the people from rejecting the prophethood and the covenant that is with you in the Torah, while you yourselves have forgotten it — you have breached My covenant and rejected what you know to be in My Book.

The verse closes on the two words that measure the offence: while you recite the Scripture, and: do you not have sense? The recitation of the Scripture is stated as the aggravation, not the excuse: the one who recites the Book knows what it commands, and the knowledge is what makes the leaving what it commands a leaving with open eyes. The same condemnation stands in the sūrah of the Ranks, in the word that gathers the whole matter: (61:3 — **“How despicable it is in the sight of Allah that you say what you do not do”**).

**THE READINGS OF THE FORGETTING**

The early scholars differed over the good the addressed commanded and forgot, and the reports carry the readings. Ibn ʿAbbās said, in the report of Ibn Isḥāq through ʿIkrimah or Saʿīd ibn Jubayr: do you command the people with the good, and forget yourselves, while you recite the Book, do you not then understand — that is, you forbid the people the disbelief, by what is with you of the prophecy and the covenant of the Torah, and you leave yourselves — meaning, while you disbelieve in what is in it of My covenant to you of the affirmation of My Messenger, and you break My covenant, and you deny what you know of My Book. And al-Ḍaḥḥāk carries from Ibn ʿAbbās: do you command the people with the good — do you command the people to enter the religion of Muḥammad ﷺ, and the rest of what you were commanded of the establishing of the prayer — and you forget yourselves?

The verse's word of the good has its breadth: the people of interpretation agreed that every obedience to God is named the good. And Abū al-Dardāʾ gives the verse its discipline of the self, in what Abū Qilābah carries: the man does not understand with the whole understanding, until he hates the people for the sake of God, then returns to himself and is more hating of it. And the verse's clauses are read one by one: al-Ḍaḥḥāk carries from Ibn ʿAbbās, and you recite the Book — you study the Book with that, and He means by the Book the Torah; and do you not then understand — do you not then comprehend? And He forbade them this ugly character.

Al-Ṭabarī draws from the verse its help: if it is asked, what is the meaning of the command to seek help by the prayer for the obedience of God, and the leaving of His disobedience, and the stripping of the leadership, and the leaving of the world? It is said: in the prayer is the recitation of the Book of God, whose verses call to the rejection of the world and the abandoning of its delight, and console the souls from its ornament and its deception, and remind of the Hereafter and what God prepared in it for its people; and in the consideration of it is the help for the people of the obedience of God upon the seriousness in it — as was reported of our Prophet ﷺ that when a matter distressed him, he fled to the prayer; and Ḥudhayfah carries it: the Messenger of God ﷺ, when a matter distressed him, fled to the prayer.

---

## Verse 2:45

> And seek help through patience and prayer. Indeed, it is a burden except for the humble—

**THE TWO HELPERS**

The address now gives the two means by which the commands it has laid are carried, and it gives them in one word: seek help in patience and the prayer. The seeking of help is the standing posture of the servant before the weight of the religion, and the two helpers are the two that the Book pairs wherever the weight is named. Muqātil ibn Ḥayyān said the verse means: use patience and the obligatory prayer in seeking the Hereafter; and the early scholars said that the patience here is the fasting, and similar texts are reported from Mujāhid — which is why Ramadan is called the month of patience, as the reports mention. It was also said that the patience here is the refraining from evil, and the word is joined to the prayer because the patience holds the servant back from what is forbidden and the prayer brings him forward to what is commanded. The prayer is thus named a helper because it is itself a prevention, as the Book states of it in the sūrah of the Spider: (29:45 — **“Indeed, ˹genuine˺ prayer should deter ˹one˺ from indecency and wickedness. The remembrance of Allah is ˹an˺ even greater ˹deterrent˺”**).

The verse then states the weight of the command: and it is heavy, except for the humble ones. Mujāhid read the pronoun as returning to the prayer, and it is also the choice of Ibn Jarīr, and it may return to the whole counsel of patience and prayer; in either reading the verse states that the religion is heavy on the soul, and that the heaviness is lifted only for one kind of heart. The word renders the khashiʿīn, the ones whose humility has broken the weight of the command for them; and Ibn ʿAbbās said, in the report of Ibn Abī Ṭalḥah, that they are those who believe in what Allah has revealed. The heaviness is thus not in the command but in the heart that meets it: the same prayer is a burden on the one who carries it with his limbs and a rest for the one who carries it with his certainty — and the verse defines that certainty in the verse that follows.

**THE WEIGHT AND THE HUMBLE**

Al-Ṭabarī fixes the verse's reference: His saying, and it — it is the prayer; the pronoun of and it returns upon the prayer. And some have said that His saying, and it, is in the meaning of the responding to Muḥammad ﷺ — and there is no mention of the responding in the wording upon which the pronoun could be made a hidden reference to it, and it is not permissible to leave the apparent, understood meaning of the speech for a hidden one with no indication of its soundness. And He means by His saying, is a heavy thing: a severe, heavy thing; and al-Ḍaḥḥāk said: it is heavy indeed.

And the exception is read in the reports by the faces of humility: al-Ṭabarī reads it: except upon the humble, that is, upon the submissive to His obedience, the fearing of His seizures, the affirmers of His promise and His threat. And Ibn ʿAbbās said, in the report of ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭalḥah: except upon the humble — he means, the affirmers of what God sent down; and Abū al-ʿĀliyah said: he means, the fearful; and Mujāhid said: the believers, truly. The weight of the verse is not the weight of the act but the weight of the carrying: the prayer is heavy upon the one who carries it with his heedlessness, and light upon the one whose humility has emptied him of himself — and the four readings of the humble gather the carriage: the submission of the obedience, and the fear of the seizure, and the affirmation of the promise, and the belief that is true.

---

## Verse 2:46

> those who are certain that they will meet their Lord and to Him they will return

**THE CERTAINTY THAT LIGHTENS THE BURDEN**

The verse defines the humble ones of the previous verse by their certainty, and the certainty is of two matters: that they are meeting their Lord, and that to Him they are returning. The word of the verse is the word of ẓann — they ẓann that they are meeting their Lord — and Mujāhid stated the rule of this word in the Book: every ẓann in the Qurʾān is knowledge; so the ẓann of this verse is the certainty of the meeting, not the conjecture of the doubter. The meeting is the standing before Allah on the Day, and the return is the journey of the whole life toward that standing, and the two are the first and the last of the one affair: the servant is certain that the road he walks ends at the meeting, and the certainty of the end is what makes the weight of the road light. The prayer that was heavy on the one who does not expect the meeting is light on the one who does, because the one who expects the meeting prepares for it, and the preparation is the worship, and the worship is not a weight on the one who is walking to his Lord.

The verse is the companion of the sūrah's opening: the muttaqīn of the second verse were described by the belief in the unseen, and the humble ones of this verse are described by the certainty of the meeting, and the two descriptions are the one standing seen from its two ends — the unseen believed in at the beginning is the Lord met at the end. The Book states the same hope of the meeting as the ground of the endurance: (29:5 — **“Whoever hopes for the meeting with Allah, ˹let them know that˺ Allah’s appointed time is sure to come”**). The verse closes the first movement of the address to the children of Israel: the favours remembered, the covenant renewed, the Book confirmed, the truth forbidden to be mixed or hidden, the acts commanded, the preaching exposed, and the helpers given — and now the sūrah will turn to the favours themselves and recount them one by one.

**ẒANN AS CERTAINTY**

The early scholars read the ẓann of the verse as the certainty. Abū al-ʿĀliyah said, in the report of al-Rabīʿ: who reckon that they will meet their Lord — the ẓann here is certainty. And Mujāhid said: every ẓann in the Qurʾān is certainty — indeed I reckoned, and they reckoned; and in the other report of Mujāhid: every ẓann in the Qurʾān is knowledge. And al-Suddī said: as for they reckon, they have certainty. And Ibn Zayd read it with its reason: because they did not see with the eye, their ẓann was a certainty, and it was not a ẓann of doubt; and he recited (69:20 — **“I surely knew I would face my reckoning.”**). And al-Ṭabarī states the language: the witnesses from the poems of the Arabs and their speech, upon that the ẓann is in the meaning of the certainty, are more than to be counted.

The grammar of the meeting has its own question: if it is asked, how was it said, they will meet their Lord, and the meeting ones were joined to the Lord, while it is known that its meaning is, those who reckon that they will meet their Lord? — and if the meaning is so, then it is of the speech of the Arabs the leaving of the joining and the establishing of the nūn, for the nūn is dropped and the joining is made in the names built from the verbs only when they are in the meaning of the past verb; but when they are in the meaning of the doing and the doer, their affair is the establishing of the nūn and the leaving of the joining — the reading of the verse stands upon the meeting as the standing of the doer, and the pronoun of the verse carries the two faces together: the certainty of the meeting, and the grammar that keeps the meeting present.

---

## Verse 2:47

> O Children of Israel! Remember ˹all˺ the favours I granted you and how I honoured you above the others

**THE HOUSE REMEMBERED**

The address returns to its opening command with a new depth: remember My favour which I bestowed upon you, and that I preferred you above the worlds. The favour is named in the singular because it is one favour in its source although many in its kinds, and the preference is named as the standing of the house in its time. Allah reminds the Children of Israel of the favours He granted their fathers and grandfathers, and of the preference by which He preferred them: He sent messengers from among them and revealed Books to them, more than any of the previous nations. The early scholars read the preference as the preference of its time: Qatādah said that He preferred them above the worlds of that age, and Mujāhid said: above the ones who were living among them; and Abū al-ʿĀliyah, in the report of al-Rabīʿ ibn Anas, said the preference is the kingship, the messengers and the Books that were granted to them instead of being granted to the other kingdoms that existed in their time, for every period has its nation. The Book states the same choice in the sūrah of the Smoke: (44:32 — **“And indeed, We chose the Israelites knowingly above the others”**), and it states it in the word of Mūsā to his people: (5:20 — **“Remember Allah’s favours upon you when He raised prophets from among you, made you sovereign, and gave you what He had never given anyone in the world”**).

The preference is the favour, and the favour is the argument: the verse is not a decoration of the house but a placing of the weight upon it. The house that was preferred with the messengers and the Books is the house that is now addressed to believe in the last messenger and the last Book, and the preference of yesterday is the obligation of today. The sūrah will gather the favours one by one in the verses that follow — the deliverance, the sea, the forgiveness, the shade, the provision, the raising — and each favour will stand as a witness of the Giver and an argument against the forgetting.

**THE PREFERRING AND ITS TIME**

Al-Ṭabarī reads the preferring as the fathers' favour standing for the sons: this too is of what God reminds them of, of His graces and His favors upon them; and His saying, and that I preferred you above the worlds — that is, I preferred your ancestors; and He ascribed His favors upon their fathers and their ancestors as favors from Him upon them, since the glories of the fathers are glories for the sons, and the favors with the fathers are favors with the sons, for the sons are from the fathers. And He brought His saying, and that I preferred you above the worlds, out in the form of the general, while He intends by it the particular: the meaning is, I preferred you above the world in whose two sides you were, and in whose time you were.

The reports agree upon the time. Qatādah said: He preferred them above the world of that time. And Abū al-ʿĀliyah said: by what they were given of the dominion and the messengers and the Books, above the world of the one who was in that time — for every time has a world. And Mujāhid said: above the ones among whom they were. And Ibn Zayd read it with the Book's own weighing: the world of the people of that time; and he recited (44:32 — **“And indeed, We chose the Israelites knowingly above the others”**), and said: this is for the one who obeyed Him and followed His command; and there were among them the apes, and they were the most hateful of His creation to Him; and He said to this community (3:110 — **“You are the best community ever raised for humanity—you encourage good, forbid evil, and believe in Allah”**), and said: this is for the one who obeyed God and followed His command and avoided His prohibitions. The preferring of the verse is a preferring of the time, and its condition is the obedience — and the verse of the community carries the same condition, so that no lineage stands without the deed.

---

## Verse 2:48

> Guard yourselves against the Day on which no soul will be of help to another. No intercession will be accepted, no ransom taken, and no help will be given

**THE DAY THAT STRIPS THE RELIANCE**

The favour is followed by the warning, and the warning is the Day. The verse commands the fear of the Day, and it describes the Day by four negations: no soul will avail another soul anything, no intercession will be accepted, no ransom will be taken, and they will not be helped. The four negations are the stripping of the four reliances the sons of Adam lean on before the Day: the availing of the fathers and the great, the intercession of the intermediaries, the ransom of the wealth, and the help of the allies. The verse is addressed to the house that relied on the standing of its fathers and on the intercession of its prophets, and it states that the Day is the Day of the single soul: the soul stands alone before its Lord, and what avails it is only what it carried. Ibn Jarīr gathered the sense of the closing negation: they will not be helped means that on that Day they shall be helped by no helper, and no one shall intercede on their behalf, and no repeal or ransom shall be accepted for them.

The same standing of the Day is stated in the sūrah of the Cleaving: (82:19 — **“˹It is˺ the Day no soul will be of ˹any˺ benefit to another whatsoever, for all authority on that Day belongs to Allah ˹entirely˺”**), and the same law of the single burden is stated in the sūrah of the Angels: (35:18 — **“No soul burdened with sin will bear the burden of another”**). The verse is the first warning of the Day in the address to the children of Israel, and it is placed directly after the reminding of the favours because the favour that is not thanked becomes the argument on the Day — and the Day is the Day on which the arguments are settled.

**THE CURRENCY OF THE DAY**

The Messenger of God ﷺ gives the Day its currency, in the reports the verse carries. Abū Hurayrah carries: God have mercy upon a servant who had, for his brother, a wrong, of honour or of wealth or of rank, and he asked him to absolve him of it before it is taken from him, while there is there no dinar and no dirham — for if he has good deeds, they will take of his good deeds, and if he has no good deeds, they will load upon him of their evil deeds. And Ibn ʿAbbās carries: let none of you die while he owes a debt, for there is there no dinar and no dirham — there they divide the good deeds and the evil deeds; and the Messenger of God ﷺ pointed with his hand to the right and to the left. And ʿUthmān ibn ʿAffān carries: the hornless sheep will take revenge from the horned, on the Day of Resurrection — as God said (21:47 — **“We will set up the scales of justice on the Day of Judgment, so no soul will be wronged in the least”**). God made them despair, by the verse, of what their souls had hoped of salvation from the punishment of God, by the intercession of their fathers and of the people all of them — with their denial of what they knew of the truth, and their opposition to the command of God in the following of Muḥammad ﷺ — and He informed them that nothing would profit them with Him but the repentance to Him from their disbelief.

And the verse's words are read one by one: Abū al-ʿĀliyah said, no ransom shall be taken from it — he means, the ransom; and Ibn ʿAbbās said: no substitute — and the substitute is the ransom. And al-Ṭabarī reads the closing: they shall not be helped — no helper will help them, as no intercessor will intercede for them, and no ransom and no price will be accepted from them; favoritism is void there, and the bribes and the intercessions have vanished, and the cooperation and the helping have risen between the people, and the judgment has returned to the just Compeller, with whom the intercessors and the helpers do not profit. And of the like of it is His saying (37:25 — **“˹Then they will be asked,˺ “What is the matter with you that you can no longer help each other?””**); and Ibn ʿAbbās said of it: what is the matter that you do not defend yourselves from us? — far is it! That is not for you today.

---

## Verse 2:49

> ˹Remember˺ how We delivered you from the people of Pharaoh, who afflicted you with dreadful torment, slaughtering your sons and keeping your women. That was a severe test from your Lord

**THE FIRST FAVOUR: THE DELIVERANCE**

The favours now begin their recounting, and the first of them is the deliverance. Remember when We delivered you from the people of Pharaoh: the house that is being addressed was the slaves of the house of Pharaoh, and the favour is the taking of them out of the slavery of the servant to the freedom of the servants of the Lord. The affliction is described in the verse: they were afflicting you with the evil torment — and the verse explains it: slaughtering your sons and keeping your women alive, and al-Suddī said: he set them to the filthy tasks, and he used to kill their sons and keep their women alive. The torment of the sons was the killing, and the torment of the women was the keeping, and the two were the two halves of the humiliation: the males of the house cut off and its females kept for service.

The reports give the cause of the slaughter: Pharaoh saw in his sleep a fire coming out of Jerusalem that entered the houses of the Copts of Egypt except the houses of the Children of Israel, and it was interpreted that his kingdom would be destroyed by a man from the Children of Israel, so he ordered the killing of their sons. The verse then closes on the reading of the whole affair: and in that was a mighty trial from your Lord. The trial is of two faces: the slaughter was the trial of the affliction, and the deliverance was the trial of the favour, and both are from the Lord — the servant is tested by the evil that is removed from him as he is tested by the good that is given to him. The same reminding stands in the word of Mūsā to his people in the sūrah of Ibrāhīm: (14:6 — **“Remember Allah’s favour upon you when He rescued you from the people of Pharaoh, who afflicted you with dreadful torment—slaughtering your sons and keeping your women. That was a severe test from your Lord”**).

**THE COUNSEL OF PHARAOH AND THE KNIVES**

The reports give the torment its history. Saʿīd ibn Jubayr carries from Ibn ʿAbbās: Pharaoh and his companions remembered what God had promised Ibrāhīm, His friend, of making prophets and kings from his offspring, and they took counsel and settled their affair upon sending men with the knives, going about among the Children of Israel, so that they found no newborn male but they slaughtered him; and they did so. And when they saw that the great ones of the Children of Israel were dying by their terms, and that the small ones were being slaughtered, they said: you are soon to bring the Children of Israel to nothing, and come to doing yourselves the works and the service they used to suffice you of; so kill, one year, every newborn male, and leave, one year — so their sons will be few. And ʿIkrimah carries from Ibn ʿAbbās: the soothsayers said to Pharaoh, there will be born in this year a boy who will take away your dominion; and he placed over every thousand women a hundred men, and over every hundred ten, and over every ten a man, and said: look to every pregnant woman in the city, and when she lays down her burden, look to it — if it is a male, slaughter it, and if it is a female, leave her; and that is His saying, they slaughter your sons and spare your women, and in that was a trial from your Lord, great.

And Abū al-ʿĀliyah gives the affliction its span: Pharaoh ruled them four hundred years; and the soothsayers said, there will be born this year in Egypt a boy whose hand will be your destruction; and he sent among the people of Egypt women who were midwives, and when a woman bore a boy he was brought to Pharaoh and he killed him, and he spared the girls. And al-Rabīʿ ibn Anas said the like: Pharaoh ruled them four hundred years, and there came to him one who said, there will grow up in Egypt a boy of the Children of Israel who will overcome you, and your destruction will be upon his hand. The favour of the verse stands upon the whole of it: the saving is from the dominion and the knives and the counsel together — and the trial is named great because it was the dominion that made the killing a policy.

---

## Verse 2:50

> And ˹remember˺ when We parted the sea, rescued you, and drowned Pharaoh’s people before your very eyes

**THE SEA AND THE TWO ENDS**

The second favour is the completion of the first: the parting of the sea. The verse gathers the three acts of the one event: We parted the sea for you, and We saved you, and We drowned the people of Pharaoh while you were looking on. The looking-on is the verse's addition to the saving: the Children of Israel did not merely hear of the drowning of their enemies — they saw it, so that the heart's fear of Pharaoh was removed by the eye, and the favour of the saving was completed by the witnessing of the drowning. The parting is described in the sūrah of the Poets, where the sea stood as two mountains of water: (26:63 — **“So We inspired Moses: “Strike the sea with your staff,” and the sea was split, each part was like a huge mountain”**).

Al-Suddī described the passing: when Mūsā came to the sea he struck it and it split, and every part was like a great mountain, and the Children of Israel entered, and there were in the sea twelve roads, a road for every tribe. The day of the deliverance remained a day of worship after it: it is reported that the day the Children of Israel were saved from Pharaoh was called the day of ʿĀshūrāʾ, and Imam Ahmad recorded that Ibn ʿAbbās said: the Messenger of Allah came to Madinah and found the Jews fasting on the day of ʿĀshūrāʾ, and he asked them, what is this day that you fast? They said, this is a good day, in which Allah saved the Children of Israel from their enemy, and Mūsā used to fast it; and the Messenger of Allah ﷺ said, I have more right to Mūsā than you have, and he fasted it and ordered that it be fasted — the ḥadīth is also recorded by al-Bukhārī, Muslim, al-Nasāʾī and Ibn Mājah.

The verse is the standing proof of the sūrah's argument with the house of Israel: the same Lord who parted the sea for the fathers is the Lord who addresses the children, and the same power that drowned Pharaoh is the power that stands behind the warning of the Day. The sea was the grave of the tyrant and the road of the slaves, and the one water carried the two ends of the affair in the one event — and the story is the story the Qurʾān tells more than any other story, because it is the story of the deliverance and its forgetting.

**THE NIGHT JOURNEY AND THE PARTING**

The reports carry the night of the parting. Ibn ʿAbbās said, in the report of ʿIkrimah: God revealed to Mūsā, set forth with My servants by night, for you will be followed; and Mūsā set forth with the Children of Israel by night, and Pharaoh followed them with a thousand thousand horse, besides the females, and Mūsā was in six hundred thousand; and when Pharaoh saw them he said (26:54 — **“˹and said,˺ “These ˹outcasts˺ are just a handful of people”**); and Mūsā set forth with the Children of Israel until they came down upon the sea, and they turned, and there was the dust of the beasts of Pharaoh, and they said: O Mūsā, we were harmed before you came to us, and after you have come to us — this is the sea before us, and this is Pharaoh who has followed us.

And al-Suddī carries the detail: God commanded Mūsā to set forth with the Children of Israel, and he said, set forth with My servants by night, for you will be followed; and Mūsā and Hārūn went forth in their people, and death was cast upon the Copts, and every firstborn of a man died, and they rose up in the morning burying them, and they were busied from the pursuit of them until the sun rose; and that is when God says (26:60 — **“And so they pursued them at sunrise”**); and Mūsā was over the rear of the Children of Israel, and Hārūn was before them leading them; and the believer said to Mūsā, O prophet of God, where have you been commanded? He said: the sea — and he wished to plunge in, and Mūsā held him back; and Mūsā went forth in six hundred thousand and twenty thousand fighters, not counting the son of twenty for his smallness, and not the son of sixty for his greatness, and counting only what was between that, besides the offspring.

And al-Qurṭubī gives the parting its language, and the day its fast: We parted, that is, We split, and every part was as the great mountain — that is, the great hill; and the root of the parting is the separation, and from it is the parting of the hair, and from it is the Furqān, because it parts between the truth and the false; and of the day, he carries: the apparent of these ḥadīths indicates that the Prophet ﷺ fasted ʿĀshūrāʾ and commanded its fast in the following of Mūsā, upon what the Jews informed of — and it is not so, for what ʿĀʾishah carried, may God be pleased with her: the day of ʿĀshūrāʾ was fasted by Quraysh in the Ignorance, and the Messenger of God ﷺ used to fast it in the Ignorance; and when he came to Madinah he fasted it and commanded its fast; and when Ramaḍān was made obligatory he left the fast of the day of ʿĀshūrāʾ, and whoever wished fasted it and whoever wished left it — reported by al-Bukhārī and Muslim.

---

## Verse 2:51

> And ˹remember˺ when We appointed forty nights for Moses, then you worshipped the calf in his absence, acting wrongfully

**THE APPOINTMENT AND THE FALL**

The third favour begins with the appointment: We appointed Mūsā forty nights. The appointment was the meeting of Mūsā with his Lord to receive the covenant, and the sūrah of the Heights states its form: (7:142 — **“We appointed for Moses thirty nights then added another ten—completing his Lord’s term of forty nights”**). Abū al-ʿĀliyah said, in the report of al-Rabīʿ ibn Anas, that the forty nights are Dhū al-Qaʿdah with ten days of Dhū al-Ḥijjah: that was when Mūsā left his companions behind and placed Hārūn over them, and he remained on the Mount forty nights, and the Torah was sent down to him on tablets — and this was after the Children of Israel were delivered from Pharaoh and had crossed the sea. The verse then states what the people did in the absence of their prophet: and you took the calf, while you were wrongdoers. The fall is stated with its full weight: the house that had just seen the sea part and Pharaoh drown took, in the forty nights of its prophet's meeting with its Lord, a calf of gold for worship.

The verse is the first of the sūrah's recountings of the house's falls, and it is placed immediately after the favours because the argument of the address is the argument of the favours against the falls. The story of the calf stands in its full form in the sūrah of the Heights and in the sūrah of Ṭā Hā, where the trial is named and its doer is named: (20:85 — **“We have indeed tested your people in your absence, and the Sâmiri has led them astray”**). The verse closes on the naming of the act: you were wrongdoers — the wronging is of the self before it is of the command, and the sūrah will return to the word in the repentance that follows.

**THE FORTY NIGHTS AND THE HASTENING**

The readers read the appointment in two ways. Abū ʿAmr read it, We promised Mūsā, without the alif, and Abū ʿUbayd chose it and preferred it, and denied the mutual promising: the mutual promising is only between humans, but God is alone with the promise and the threat; upon that the Qurʾān is found — His saying (14:22 — **“Indeed, Allah has made you a true promise. I too made you a promise, but I failed you”**), and His saying (8:7 — **“˹Remember, O believers,˺ when Allah promised ˹to give˺ you the upper hand over either target, you wished to capture the unarmed party”**). And al-Ṭabarī states the right of the two readings: they are two readings the community has brought, and the readers have read with both, and there is not in the reading of one of them the voiding of the meaning of the other — for the one who reports of a person that he promised another the meeting at a place, it is known that the one promised that is the promiser of his companion, of his meeting him at that place, with the like of what his companion promised him of that, when his promising him what he promised of that was by agreement of the two of them upon it; and it is not doubted of Mūsā that he was pleased with all that God commanded him of it, and hastened to His love in it.

Abū al-ʿĀliyah gives the nights their names, in the report of al-Rabīʿ ibn Anas: He means Dhū al-Qaʿdah, and ten of Dhū al-Ḥijjah — and that was when Mūsā left his companions behind and placed Hārūn over them, and he stayed upon the Ṭūr forty nights, and the Torah was sent down upon him in the tablets — and the tablets were of hail — and the Lord brought him near as one spoken with in secret, and spoke to him, and he heard the scratching of the pen; and it reached us that he did not commit an act in the forty nights, until he descended from the Ṭūr. And Ibn Zayd gives the hastening its heart: when God saved the Children of Israel from Pharaoh and drowned Pharaoh and those with him, Mūsā said to his brother Hārūn, be my successor in my people, and put right, and do not follow the way of the corrupters; and when Mūsā went out and commanded Hārūn with what he commanded him, he went out hastening, rejoicing, to God — for Mūsā knew that the man, when he succeeds in the need of his master, it gladdens him that he hasten to him.

---

## Verse 2:52

> Even then We ˹still˺ forgave you so perhaps you would be grateful

**THE FAVOUR AFTER THE FALL**

The verse states the favour that follows the fall: then We forgave you after that, so that perhaps you would be thankful. Ibn ʿAbbās read the pardon as the leaving of the house alive: We forgave you means We left you alone and did not exterminate you, so that you would give thanks for the pardoning. The forgiveness is named as the favour, and it is the greater of the two favours of the passage: the favour of the appointment was the gift of the covenant, and the favour of the forgiveness was the gift of the standing after the covenant was broken. The verse is the first appearance in the sūrah of the pattern that the whole address is built on: the fall of the house is followed by the forgiveness of the Lord, and the forgiveness is given its purpose — that perhaps you would be thankful. The thank is the standing the forgiveness returns the forgiven one to: the house that was forgiven the calf was returned to the gratitude of the deliverance, and the gratitude is the worship of the One who forgave.

The Book states the same forgiveness in the sūrah of the Women, in the recounting of the same fall: (4:153 — **“Still We forgave them for that ˹after their repentance˺ and gave Moses compelling proof”**). The verse is the standing lesson of the house's story for the community that reads it: the fall is not the end of the affair with Allah — the repentance is the gate, and the forgiveness is the favour, and the thank is the return.

**THE ORIGIN OF THE CALF**

Ibn ʿAbbās gives the calf its whole story, in the report of ʿIkrimah: when Pharaoh came down upon the sea, he and his companions — and Pharaoh was upon a black stallion, tall, a horse — and when he came down upon the sea, the horse feared to plunge into the sea; and Jibrīl appeared to him upon a female horse in heat, and when the stallion saw her he plunged after her. And the Sāmirī recognized Jibrīl, because his mother, when she feared he would be slaughtered, left him in a cave and closed it over him, and Jibrīl used to come to him and feed him with his fingers — and he found in some of his fingers milk, and in another honey, and in another butter — and he did not cease feeding him until he grew up; and when he saw him with his own eyes in the sea he knew him, and he took a handful of the track of his horse. And it was cast into the heart of the Sāmirī that you will not cast it upon a thing and say, be so and so, but it will be; and the handful did not cease to be in his hand until he crossed the sea. And when Mūsā and the Children of Israel crossed the sea, and God drowned the people of Pharaoh, Mūsā said to his brother Hārūn, be my successor in my people, and put right; and Mūsā went on to the appointment of his Lord. And with the Children of Israel was jewellery of the jewellery of the people of Pharaoh that they had borrowed, and it was as if they held it a sin, and they brought it out for the fire to come down and eat it; and when they had gathered it, the Sāmirī did thus with the handful that was in his hand, and cast it into it, and said, be a calf, a body, with a lowing; and it became a calf, a body, with a lowing — and the wind entered into its hinder part and came out of its mouth, and a sound was heard of it.

Al-Qurṭubī gives the pardon its language: the pardon is the pardon of God upon His creation, and it may be after the punishment and before it, unlike the forgiveness, with which there is no punishment at all; and every one who deserved a punishment and it was left for him, he has been pardoned; so the pardon is the effacement of the sin — We effaced your sins and passed over you — taken from your saying, the wind has effaced the track, that is, made it go. The favour of the verse is the standing of the people after the effacement: the calf was burned and the sin was effaced, and the people were left alive to give thanks.

---

## Verse 2:53

> And ˹remember˺ when We gave Moses the Scripture—the standard ˹to distinguish between right and wrong˺ that perhaps you would be ˹rightly˺ guided

**THE GIFT OF THE BOOK**

The favours continue with the greatest of them: the Book. Remember when We gave Mūsā the Book and the criterion, so that perhaps you would be guided. The Book is the Torah, and the criterion is the furqān — the discernment between the truth and the falsehood. Mujāhid read the two words as one: the Book, he said, is itself the furqān — the criterion between the truth and the falsehood; and others read them as the two names of the one gift, or as the Book and its character: the Book that was given to Mūsā was given with its furqān, because the Book is the criterion by which the truth is separated from the falsehood. The gift is given its purpose in the verse: that perhaps you would be guided — the Book was not given for the keeping but for the guidance, and the guidance is the walking by the Book, not the possessing of it.

The same gift is stated in the sūrah of the Prophets, with the same two names and their light: (21:48 — **“Indeed, We granted Moses and Aaron the standard ˹to distinguish between right and wrong˺—a light and a reminder for the righteous”**). The verse is the house's deepest favour and its deepest argument: the house that was given the criterion is the house that is now called to judge by it in the matter of this Book — and the criterion that judges between the truth and the falsehood will judge for the truth that this Book carries. The sūrah opened with the same description of its own Book: a Book in which there is no doubt, a guidance for the muttaqīn — and the Torah was given with the same description, so that the two Books stand as the one argument from the one Lord.

**THE BOOK AND THE FURQĀN**

Al-Ṭabarī reads the two gifts: He means by the Book the Torah, and by the Furqān the separation between the truth and the false. And Abū al-ʿĀliyah said: He separated by it between the truth and the false; and Mujāhid said: the Book is the Furqān — a separation between the truth and the false. And Ibn ʿAbbās gives the Furqān its width, in the report of Ibn Jurayj: the Furqān is the gathering name of the Torah and the Gospel and the Psalms and the Furqān.

And Ibn Zayd reads the Furqān by the Book's own Day, in the report of Ibn Wahb: as for the Furqān of which God said (8:41 — **“if you ˹truly˺ believe in Allah and what We revealed to Our servant on that decisive day when the two armies met ˹at Badr˺”**) — that is the Day of Badr, the day God separated between the truth and the false, and the judgement by which He separated between the truth and the false; so likewise God gave Mūsā the Furqān — God separated between them, and saved him and delivered him, separating between them with the victory; and as God made that between Muḥammad ﷺ and the polytheists, so He made it between Mūsā and Pharaoh. The two readings stand together: the Furqān is the Book's own separating, and the separating is the victory that carries the Book — and the guidance of the verse is the standing of the two upon the people who received them.

---

## Verse 2:54

> And ˹remember˺ when Moses said to his people, “O my people! Surely you have wronged yourselves by worshipping the calf, so turn in repentance to your Creator and execute ˹the calf-worshippers among˺ yourselves. That is best for you in the sight of your Creator.” Then He accepted your repentance. Surely He is the Accepter of Repentance, Most Merciful

**THE REPENTANCE OF THE CALF**

The verse recounts the repentance that followed the fall, and it is the heaviest repentance in the Book. Mūsā said to his people: O my people, you have wronged yourselves by taking the calf, so turn in repentance to your Creator, and kill yourselves. The early scholars read the killing as the executing of the wrongdoers by the innocent among them: the ones who had not worshipped the calf stood against the ones who had, and the repentance of the house was the sword of the house. Saʿīd ibn Jubayr and Mujāhid described the scene: some of them rose upon some with daggers, killing one another, and no man spared a kinsman near or far, until Mūsā waved with his garment signalling them to stop. Al-Ḥasan al-Baṣrī said: when their hearts thought of worshipping the calf, and when they regretted and saw that they had gone astray, they said: (7:149 — **“If our Lord does not have mercy on us and forgive us, we will certainly be losers”**) — this is when Mūsā said to them: you have wronged yourselves by taking the calf. The verse measures the command with its end: that is better for you with your Creator — the killing of the wrongdoers by the hands of the house was better with Allah than the standing of the house in its sin, and the severity of the repentance was the measure of the severity of the fall.

The verse then states the acceptance: then He turned to you in mercy — and it closes on the two names by which the repentance of Adam closed: He is the Accepter of repentance, the Most Merciful. The two repentances stand together as the two poles of the sūrah's teaching of the repentance: the repentance of the father was by the words he was taught, and the repentance of the house was by the sword it was commanded, and both were accepted by the same two names. The verse is the standing proof that the door of the repentance is not closed after the greatest of the falls — the calf was the greatest of the falls of the house, and the repentance after it was accepted.

**THE SCENE OF THE KILLING**

The reports carry the scene of the repentance in its detail.
