Ibn ʿAbbās said, in the report of ʿIkrimah: Mūsā commanded his people, by the command of their Lord, to kill themselves; and those who had kept vigil over the calf sat down, having girded themselves, and those who had not kept vigil over the calf stood up and took the daggers in their hands; and a severe darkness fell upon them, and they fell to killing one another; and the darkness cleared from them when they had swept over seventy thousand slain — every one of them who was killed had a repentance, and every one who remained had a repentance. And Mujāhid said: Mūsā commanded his people, by the command of his Lord, that some of them kill some with the daggers, and the man fell to killing his father and killing his child; and God turned to them. And Abū al-ʿĀliyah said: they became two ranks, and they fell to killing one another, and the slain reached what God willed; then it was said to them, the repentance has been accepted, of the killer and the killed.

And Ibn Shihāb carries the standing of Mūsā in the scene: when the Children of Israel were commanded with the killing of themselves, they came out, and Mūsā was with them, and they clashed with the swords and thrust with the daggers, while Mūsā was raising his two hands; and when he flagged, some of them came to him and said, O prophet of God, call upon God for us — and they took his two arms and supported his two hands; and their affair did not cease to be so, until when God accepted their repentance He held back the hands of some of them from some, and they cast the weapons; and Mūsā and the Children of Israel were grieved for the killing that had been among them, and God revealed to Mūsā: what grieves you? — as for the one who was killed of you, he is alive with Me, provided for; and as for the one who remained, I have accepted his repentance. So Mūsā gave the Children of Israel the good news of that. And ʿAṭāʾ carries from ʿUbayd ibn ʿUmayr: some of them stood to some, killing one another — the man did not spare his brother, nor his father, nor his son, nor anyone — until the repentance came down. And Abū al-ʿĀliyah reads the returning: then repent to your Maker — that is, to your Creator. The repentance of the verse was not a word but a scene, and the verse records both faces of it — the killing that took the sin out of the camp, and the turning that made the killer and the killed equal in the acceptance.

---

## Verse 2:55

> And ˹remember˺ when you said, “O Moses! We will never believe you until we see Allah with our own eyes,” so a thunderbolt struck you while you were looking on

**THE DEMAND AND THE THUNDERBOLT**

The verse recounts the second fall of the house after the sea. The Children of Israel said to their prophet: we will not believe in you until we see Allah openly. The demand was the demand of the seeing of the Lord with the eyes, and the Book states that the seeing was refused to Mūsā himself at the mount, and the seventy of the house who were chosen for the meeting demanded what their prophet was refused. Ibn Jurayj said that the verse reminds the house of the favour of their resurrection: remember My favour upon you in that I raised you after the thunderbolt took you, when you asked to see Me openly, which neither you nor anyone else can bear or attain. The verse describes the taking: the thunderbolt took you while you were looking on, and the early scholars read the looking-on: some of them were struck with the thunderbolt while the others were looking, then Allah raised those and struck the others — so the house saw the death of its chosen ones and saw their raising, one man at a time, while the rest looked on.

The report states that the seventy men were among those who had worshipped the calf, and that Allah brought them back to life one by one while their brothers watched how Allah brings the dead to life; and al-Rabīʿ ibn Anas said: the death was their punishment, and they were raised after they died to complete their lives, and Qatādah said the same. The verse is the house's own proof against the house: the ones who demanded to see Allah to believe were shown the raising of the dead among them, and the faith that was refused to the seeing was given to the raising. The Book recounts the same demand and the same thunderbolt in the sūrah of the Women: (4:153 — **“They demanded what is even greater than this from Moses, saying, “Make Allah visible to us!” So a thunderbolt struck them for their wrongdoing”**).

**THE OPEN SEEING AND THE REMEMBERING**

Al-Ṭabarī gives the open seeing its language: remember when you said, O Mūsā, we will not believe you, and will not affirm what you have brought us, until we see God openly — with the eye, by the raising of the veil between us and Him, and the uncovering of the cover between us and Him, until we look at Him with our eyes — as the well is opened, when its water has been covered by the mud, and what covered it is cleaned until the water appears and is clear; one says, I have opened the well, I open it, an opening; and for that it is said, so-and-so has been open with this matter, when he has shown it to the sight of the eye and declared it. And Ibn ʿAbbās said, in the report of Ibn Jurayj: openly; and al-Rabīʿ said: with the eye; and Qatādah said: with the eye.

Al-Ṭabarī gives the remembering its purpose: God reminded them, by that, of the differing of their fathers, and the evil of the uprightness of their ancestors with their prophets, with the abundance of what they witnessed of the signs of God and His lessons, whose least would cool the breasts and settle the souls with affirmation — while the arguments followed upon them, and the favors of God were poured upon them; and they, with that, asked their prophet once to make them a god other than God, and worshipped the calf once, other than God, and said once, we will not believe you until we see God openly; and another time, when they were called to the fighting, they said to him, go, you and your Lord, and fight — we are sitting here; and it was said to them once, say the remission, and enter the gate prostrating, We will forgive you your sins — and they said, wheat in barley! And our Lord informed the ones He addressed with these verses, of the Jews of the Children of Israel who were between the two sides of the place of the emigration of the Messenger of God ﷺ, that they would not exceed, in their denial of Muḥammad ﷺ and their rejection of his prophethood and their leaving of the affirmation of him and of what he brought, with their knowledge of him and their knowledge of the reality of his affair — to be as their ancestors and their fathers, whose story He detailed upon them, of their apostasy from their religion, time after time, and their leaping upon their prophet Mūsā, once after once, with the greatness of the trial of God upon them, and the pouring of His favors upon them.

---

## Verse 2:56

> Then We brought you back to life after your death, so that perhaps you would be grateful

**THE FAVOUR OF THE RAISING**

The verse completes the story of the thunderbolt with its second half, and the second half is the favour. The death by the thunderbolt was the punishment of the demand, and the raising after it was the gift of the Giver: then We raised you after your death, so that perhaps you would be thankful. The report states the manner of the raising: Allah revealed to Mūsā that the seventy men were among those who had worshipped the calf, and Allah brought them back to life one man at a time while the rest of them looked on at how Allah brings the dead to life. Al-Rabīʿ ibn Anas read the death and the raising as the punishment and the reprieve: the death was their punishment, and they were raised after they died so that they could complete their lives; and Qatādah said the same. The verse thus gathers the two standings of the house in the one event: the ones who demanded the seeing were shown the raising, and the death that was the end of their demand became the beginning of their gratitude.

The thank of the verse is the standing the raising returns them to, as the thank of the previous forgiveness returned the house to its gratitude. The Book recounts the demand and the thunderbolt in the sūrah of the Women, where the same demand is named greater than what the house of this sūrah was asked to carry: (4:153 — **“They demanded what is even greater than this from Moses, saying, “Make Allah visible to us!” So a thunderbolt struck them for their wrongdoing”**), and the vision that was refused at the mount is described in the sūrah of the Heights, where the mountain itself could not bear the appearing of the Lord: (7:143 — **“When his Lord appeared to the mountain, He levelled it to dust and Moses collapsed unconscious”**). The verse is the standing proof of the sūrah that the seeing of Allah is not the ground of the faith: the house that was refused the seeing was given the raising, and the raising is the greater proof — and the faith that refuses the proof of the raising will have no proof to stand on.

**THE SEVENTY AND THE THUNDERBOLT**

The reports give the thunderbolt its occasion. Ibn Isḥāq carries: when Mūsā returned to his people and saw what they were in, of the worship of the calf, and said to his brother and to the Sāmirī what he said, and burned the calf and scattered it in the sea, Mūsā chose from among them seventy men, the best and the best, and said, set out. And Ibn Zayd gives the scene its word: Mūsā said to them, when he returned from his Lord with the tablets, in which the Torah was written, and found them worshipping the calf, and commanded them with the killing of themselves, and they did it, and God turned to them: these tablets are in them the Book of God, in them His command with which He commanded you, and His prohibition with which He prohibited you; and they said, who will take it by your saying, you? — no, by God, until we see God openly, until God rises to us and says, this is My Book, take it; and what is it that He does not speak to us as He spoke to you, O Mūsā, and says, this is My Book, take it? — and he recited the saying of God, we will not believe you until we see God openly; and there came a wrath from God, and there came to them a thunderbolt, after the repentance, and it thunderstruck them, and they died, all of them; then He revived them.

And al-Rabīʿ ibn Anas reads the verse by its people: they are the seventy whom Mūsā chose, and they journeyed with him; and they heard a speech, and said, we will not believe you until we see God openly; and they heard a sound, and they were struck — that is, they died; and that is His saying, then We raised you after your death — and they were raised after their death, because that death of theirs was a punishment for them, and they were raised for the remainder of their terms. And al-Ṭabarī settles the report of the occasion: there is no report with us of the soundness of anything of what the ones we mentioned said, of the cause of their saying that to Mūsā, by which an argument stands and it is submitted to; and it is possible that it was some of what they said — so the right of the saying in it is to say that God informed about the people of Mūsā that they said to him, O Mūsā, we will not believe you until we see God openly, as He informed that they said it; and God informed the ones addressed with these verses of that only as a reproach of them in their disbelief in Muḥammad ﷺ, after His argument had been established upon them — and there is no need, for the one the report reaches, of the knowledge of the cause that called them to the saying of that.

---

## Verse 2:57

> And ˹remember when˺ We shaded you with clouds and sent down to you manna and quails, ˹saying˺, “Eat from the good things We have provided for you.” The evildoers ˹certainly˺ did not wrong Us, but wronged themselves

**THE PROVISION OF THE WANDERING**

The favours continue through the years of the wandering, and the verse gives the two provisions of the wilderness: the shade and the food. We shaded you with the clouds: the white clouds that shaded the Children of Israel from the heat of the sun during their years of wandering, as Ibn ʿAbbās stated in the ḥadīth of the trials recorded by al-Nasāʾī, and the like is reported from Ibn ʿUmar, al-Rabīʿ ibn Anas, Abū Mijlaz, al-Ḍaḥḥāk and al-Suddī. And We sent down upon you the manna and the quails: the manna used to descend upon them to the trees, and they ate from it, and the quails — the salwā — are the birds that were driven to them. Ibn ʿAbbās said, in the report of ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭalḥah, that the salwā is a bird that looks like the quail, and the same was said by Mujāhid, al-Shaʿbī, al-Ḍaḥḥāk, al-Ḥasan, ʿIkrimah and al-Rabīʿ ibn Anas; and ʿIkrimah said it is a bird of Paradise about the size of a sparrow.

The provision is given with its word: eat of the good things We have provided for you. The eating is the permission and the command together, and the good things are described as provided — the rizq is the standing word of the gift, and the verse names the Giver in the provision. The verse then closes on the law of the whole passage: they did not wrong Us, but they wronged themselves. The wronging is returned upon its doers: the rebellion against the favour does not reach the Giver of the favour, and it returns on the one who rebels with its own weight. The same favours stand together in the sūrah of the Heights: (7:160 — **“We shaded them with clouds and sent down to them manna and quails, ˹saying˺, “Eat from the good things We have provided for you.” They ˹certainly˺ did not wrong Us, but wronged themselves”**), and they are stated in the address of the sūrah of Ṭā Hā: (20:80 — **“and sent down to you manna and quails”**). The verse is the house's provision and the house's argument together: the wilderness that had nothing gave them everything, and the Giver of everything is the One they are now asked to believe in.

**THE CLOUD AND THE MANNA IN THE REPORTS**

The reports give the shading and the provision their description. Mujāhid said: We shaded you with the cloud — it is not the cloud; and in his other report: it is not the cloud — it is the cloud in which God comes on the Day of Resurrection, and it was for none but them; and in a third report of his: it is of the kind of the cloud. And Ibn ʿAbbās said: it is a cloud colder than this and more pleasant, and it is the one in which God comes on the Day of Resurrection, in His saying, in shades of the cloud, and it is the one in which the angels came on the Day of Badr; and Ibn ʿAbbās said: and it was with them in the wilderness.

And the manna is read in the reports by its kinds. Mujāhid said: the manna is a gum; and al-Rabīʿ ibn Anas said: the manna is a drink that used to come down upon them, like honey, and they mixed it with water, then drank it; and Ibn ʿAbbās said: the manna used to come down upon their trees, and they rose in the morning to it and ate of it what they wished. The wilderness was emptied of every provision, and the cloud and the manna filled it — and the verse sets the two favours beside the demand of the verse before it, so that the seeing that was refused is measured against the shading that was given.

---

## Verse 2:58

> And ˹remember˺ when We said, “Enter this city and eat freely from wherever you please; enter the gate with humility, saying, ‘Absolve us.’ We will forgive your sins and multiply the reward for the good-doers.”

**THE CITY, THE GATE AND THE WORD**

The favours now pass from the wilderness to the city, and the verse gives the command of the entrance. The city is the holy land the house was carried toward, and the correct opinion in the early scholars is that it is Jerusalem: al-Suddī, al-Rabīʿ ibn Anas, Qatādah and Abū Muslim al-Iṣfahānī said it is Bayt al-Maqdis, while Ibn ʿAbbās and ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Zayd said it is Jericho; and the sūrah admonishes the house in this verse for the refusing of the entering and the fighting that was commanded with it, as the sūrah of the Table recounts: (5:21 — **“O my people! Enter the Holy Land which Allah has destined for you ˹to enter˺”**). The command of the entrance is given in its three parts: enter the city and eat of it freely wherever you wish, and enter the gate prostrating, and say: ḥiṭṭah. The gate was named by Ibn ʿAbbās, Mujāhid, al-Suddī, Qatādah and al-Ḍaḥḥāk as the gate of Ḥiṭṭah in Īliyāʾ, which is Jerusalem; and the prostration is the bowing of humility at the entering. The word of the entering is ḥiṭṭah, and Ibn ʿAbbās said it means: seek the forgiveness of Allah; and al-Ḥasan and Qatādah said it means: say, relieve us of our errors.

The promise of the entering follows the command: We will forgive you your errors, and We will increase the good-doers in good. The entrance is thus made a worship in itself: the entering of the city is joined to the bowing and the word of the forgiveness, and the reward of the three is the forgiveness and the increase. The verse closes its command on the mercy, as the sūrah closes its commands on the mercy, and the mercy is the ground of the entering as it is the ground of the eating. The Ṣaḥīḥ records how the house received the command: al-Bukhārī recorded from Abū Hurayrah that the Prophet ﷺ said: it was said to the Children of Israel, enter the gate prostrating and say ḥiṭṭah, and they entered on their behinds and changed the word, and they said: a grain in a hair — so they deviated and changed what they were commanded, and the next verse states what the changing earned them.

**THE TOWN AND THE WORD OF THE GATE**

The reports fix the town and the gate. Al-Rabīʿ said: and when We said, enter this town — he means, Bayt al-Maqdis. And Mujāhid said: enter the gate prostrating — the gate of the remission, of the gate of Īliyāʾ, of Bayt al-Maqdis; and Ibn ʿAbbās said: it is one of the gates of Bayt al-Maqdis, and it is called the Gate of the Remission; and as for His saying, prostrating — Ibn ʿAbbās used to interpret it in the meaning of bowing.

And the word of the gate is read in the reports. ʿIkrimah said: and say the remission — say, there is no god but God. Al-Ṭabarī weighs the grammar of that reading: upon the interpretation of the saying of ʿIkrimah, the right is that the reading of the remission be with the accusative, for if the people were commanded to say, there is no god but God, or to say, we ask forgiveness of God, then it has been said to them, say this word, and say falls then upon the remission — because the remission, upon the saying of ʿIkrimah, is the saying of there is no god but God, and when it is the saying of there is no god but God, the saying falls upon it, as when a man commands a man with the saying of the good and says to him, say good, with the accusative; and it would not be sound to say to him, say good, except upon a severe forcing. And in the agreement of the readers upon the raising of the remission is a clear declaration against what ʿIkrimah said of the interpretation of His saying, and say the remission; and likewise the right, upon the interpretation we reported from al-Ḥasan and Qatādah, is that the reading of the remission be with the accusative — because it is of the way of the Arabs, when they place the sources in the places of the verbs and delete the verbs, to install the sources. The agreement of the readers stands above the interpretation, and the verse keeps its word as the word the people were commanded to say.

---

## Verse 2:59

> But the wrongdoers changed the words they were commanded to say. So We sent down a punishment from the heavens upon them for their rebelliousness

**THE CHANGING AND THE PUNISHMENT**

The verse states the house's answer to the command of the entering: the wrongdoers changed the word they were commanded for another word. The changing is described as the act of the wrongdoers, and the verse of the Book does not name them except by their act: the ones who changed are the wrongdoers, and the wronging is what named them. The changing was the changing of the word and the changing of the act together, as the ḥadīth of the previous verse describes: they entered on their behinds where they were commanded to bow, and they said a grain in a hair where they were commanded to say ḥiṭṭah — the body and the tongue both turned the command into its opposite. The bowing they were commanded was the bowing of the entering, as Ibn ʿAbbās said in the report of Ibn Jarīr: enter the gate prostrating means, while bowing.

The punishment of the changing is stated in the verse: We sent down upon the wrongdoers a defilement from the heaven for their rebellion. The defilement is the rijs, the punishment sent from above, and the reason named is the rebellion: the fisq that carried the changing. The same story stands in the sūrah of the Heights with the same word of the changing and the same punishment: (7:162 — **“But the wrongdoers among them changed the words they were commanded to say. So We sent down a punishment from the heavens upon them for their wrongdoing”**). The verse is the standing lesson of the sūrah on the words of the commands: the word the servant is commanded to say is part of the command, and the changing of the word is the changing of the command, and the one who changes the word has left the obedience before he leaves the place.

**THE CHANGING IN THE REPORTS**

The Messenger of God ﷺ himself carries the changing. Abū Hurayrah reports: the Messenger of God ﷺ said: God said to the Children of Israel, enter the gate prostrating and say the remission, We will forgive you your sins — and they changed, and entered the gate crawling upon their buttocks, and they said, a grain in a barley-ear. And in the other report, from the Prophet ﷺ: they entered the gate — the one they were commanded to enter through prostrating — crawling upon their buttocks, saying, wheat in a barley-ear. And Ibn ʿAbbās carries it, through Saʿīd ibn Jubayr: prostrating — that is, bowing, from a small gate; and they fell to entering from the direction of their buttocks, saying, wheat; and that is His saying, so the ones who did wrong changed a word other than the one that was said to them; and in his other report: they were commanded to enter bowing and to say the remission — he said, they were commanded to ask forgiveness — and they fell to entering from the direction of their buttocks, from a small gate, saying, wheat, mocking.

And ʿAbd Allāh ibn Masʿūd read the word of the changing: enter the gate prostrating and say the remission — they said, red wheat in which there is barley; and God sent down, so the ones who did wrong changed a word other than the one that was said to them. And Mujāhid gives the scene its form: Mūsā commanded his people to enter the gate prostrating and to say the remission, and the gate was lowered for them so that they might prostrate, and they did not prostrate, and they entered upon their backs, and they said, wheat; and in his other report: Mūsā commanded his people to enter the place of worship and to say the remission, and the gate was lowered for them so that they might lower their heads, and they did not prostrate, and they entered upon their buttocks toward the mountain — and it is the mountain upon which his Lord manifested Himself to him — and they said, wheat; and that is the changing of which God said, so the ones who did wrong changed a word other than the one that was said to them. The changing gathered the body and the tongue: the crawling upon the buttocks changed the prostration, and the wheat changed the remission — and the verse names both by one name.

---

## Verse 2:60

> And ˹remember˺ when Moses prayed for water for his people, We said, “Strike the rock with your staff.” Then twelve springs gushed out, ˹and˺ each tribe knew its drinking place. ˹We then said,˺ “Eat and drink of Allah’s provisions, and do not go about spreading corruption in the land.”

**THE STONE AND THE TWELVE SPRINGS**

The favours of the wilderness continue with the water, and the verse gives the favour in its full form. Mūsā asked for water for his people, and the answer came in the command: strike the stone with your staff. Ibn ʿAbbās said, in the long ḥadīth of the trials that al-Nasāʾī, Ibn Jarīr and Ibn Abī Ḥātim recorded, that the Children of Israel had a square stone which Mūsā was commanded to strike with his staff, and twelve springs burst out of that stone, three on each side, and each tribe was designated its spring, and they used to drink from their springs; and wherever they camped they found the same bounty in the same manner. The twelve springs for the twelve tribes are the division of the mercy by the division of the house: every tribe knew its drinking place, and the knowing is the knowing of the assigned place, so that the drinking was the drinking of the order, not the drinking of the crowding.

The provision is then stated with its permission and its prohibition: eat and drink of what Allah has provided, and do not act corruptly in the earth making mischief. The early scholars read the prohibition as the condition of the provision: do not repay the favour with the disobedience that removes the favours. The same favour stands in the sūrah of the Heights with the same springs: (7:160 — **“Strike the rock with your staff.” Then twelve springs gushed out. Each tribe knew its drinking place”**), and the two descriptions of the gushing differ by the two moments: the sūrah of the Heights describes the first of the water's coming forth, and this sūrah describes its bursting in full force. The verse is the standing proof that the provision of the Lord is the provision of the order: the water of the stone was divided as the house was divided, and every tribe drank from its place, and the order of the drinking is the order of the obedience the verse commands.

**THE STONE OF THE WILDERNESS**

Ibn ʿAbbās gives the stone its whole furnishing, in the report of Saʿīd ibn Jubayr: that was in the wilderness — the cloud was shaded over them, and the manna and the quails were sent down upon them, and there were made for them garments that do not wear out and do not become dirty; and there was placed between their two sides a square stone, and Mūsā was commanded, and he struck the stone with his staff, and there burst out from it twelve springs — in every side of it three springs, for every tribe a spring; and they did not journey a journey but they found that stone with them, in the place where it had been with them in the first camp. And in his other report, through ʿIkrimah: that was in the wilderness; Mūsā struck the stone for them, and there came to be in it twelve springs of water, for every tribe of them a spring from which they drank; and Mujāhid said: for every tribe of them a spring — all of that was in their wilderness, when they wandered; and in his other report: they feared the thirst in their wilderness, when they wandered, and the stone burst for them with twelve springs, Mūsā struck it. And Ibn Jurayj carries from Ibn ʿAbbās the tribes: the tribes are the sons of Yaʿqūb — they were twelve men, each of whom fathered a tribe, a community of the people.

Al-Ṭabarī reads the knowledge of the drinking-place: every people knew their drinking-place — because their meaning, in what God brought out for them from the stone whose description He described in this verse, of the drinking, was contrary to the meanings of the rest of creation in what God brings out for them of the waters from the mountains and the lands, which have no owner other than God — for God had made for every tribe of the twelve tribes a spring from the stone, from which they drank, other than the rest of the tribes, and no tribe of them entered upon the drinking of another tribe; and with that, for every one of those twelve springs there was a place of the stone that the tribe from which its drinking came knew; and for that He singled them out with the report of them, that every people of them were knowing. And Abū al-ʿĀliyah reads the closing of the provision: and do not commit outrage in the earth, working corruption — do not walk in the earth with corruption. The stone is the standing argument of the verse: the wilderness has no water, and the stone gives twelve waters, and every tribe knows its own — and the one who gives the water by the tribes is the One who asks the thanks of the drinking.

---

## Verse 2:61

> And ˹remember˺ when you said, “O Moses! We cannot endure the same meal ˹every day˺. So ˹just˺ call upon your Lord on our behalf, He will bring forth for us some of what the earth produces of herbs, cucumbers, garlic, lentils, and onions.” Moses scolded ˹them˺, “Do you exchange what is better for what is worse? ˹You can˺ go down to any village and you will find what you have asked for.” They were stricken with disgrace and misery, and they invited the displeasure of Allah for rejecting Allah’s signs and unjustly killing the prophets. This is ˹a fair reward˺ for their disobedience and violations

**THE COMPLAINT AND ITS ANSWER**

The verse recounts the house's answer to the provision of the wilderness. They said to their prophet: we cannot endure one food — the one food is the manna and the quails, which they ate day after day — so call upon your Lord to bring forth for us of what the earth grows, of its herbs, its cucumbers, its fūm, its lentils and its onions. Ibn Masʿūd read the fūm as the thūm, which is the garlic, and al-Ḥasan read it likewise in the report of Ibn Abī Ḥātim. The answer of Mūsā is the answer of the prophet over the complaint: would you exchange what is better for what is lower? The manna and the quails are the better: the food sent down from the heaven, the good and pure and easily acquired, and what they asked for is the lower: the plants of the earth that grow in every town. Go down to any town, he said, and you will have what you asked for — and Ibn ʿAbbās said that miṣr here means any city, while Abū al-ʿĀliyah and al-Rabīʿ ibn Anas read it as Egypt, the Egypt of Pharaoh; and the truth is the first reading: the meaning of Mūsā's word is that what you ask for is easy and abundant in every town, so I will not ask Allah for it when it is the lower food in place of the better.

**THE HUMILIATION AND ITS REASON**

The verse then states the standing that the complaint and the rebellion earned the house: they were covered with humiliation and misery, and they returned with the wrath of Allah. Abū al-ʿĀliyah, al-Rabīʿ ibn Anas and al-Suddī said that the misery is poverty; ʿAṭiyyah al-ʿAwfī said it is the paying of the tax; and al-Ḥasan read the humiliation as the standing that remains: Allah humiliated them and there is no protector for them, and He put them under the feet of the Muslims, who appeared at the time when the Majūs were taking the jizyah from the Jews. The verse gives the reasons of the standing: that they disbelieved in the signs of Allah and killed the prophets without right, and it closes on the measure: that is for their disobedience and their transgression. The verse is the sūrah's gathering of the house's history into one verdict: the complaint against the provision, the disbelief in the signs, and the killing of the prophets are the three acts, and the humiliation and the misery and the wrath are the three fruits, and the justice of the fruits is stated in the closing words: that is for their disobedience and their transgression. The same verdict stands in the sūrah of the Family of ʿImrān, with the same fruits and the same reasons: (3:112 — **“They have invited the displeasure of Allah and have been branded with misery for rejecting Allah’s revelations and murdering ˹His˺ prophets unjustly. This is ˹a fair reward˺ for their disobedience and violations”**).

**THE ONE FOOD AND THE EXCHANGE**

Al-Ṭabarī reads the verse by its food: remember when you said — O company of the Children of Israel — we will not bear to restrain our souls upon one food; and that one food is what God fed them of it in their wilderness — and it is the quails, in the saying of some of the people of interpretation, and in the saying of Wahb ibn Munabbih it is the fine bread with the meat. And Qatādah said: the people were in the wilderness, and the cloud had been shaded over them, and the manna and the quails had been sent down upon them, and they grew weary of that, and they remembered a living they had had in Egypt, and they asked it of Mūsā; and God said, go down to a town, and you will have what you asked. And Abū al-ʿĀliyah said: their food was the quails, and their drink was the manna, and they asked what is mentioned, and it was said to them, go down to a town, and you will have what you asked. And Ibn Abī Najīḥ read it: we will not endure upon one food — the manna and the quails; and they exchanged for it the herbs and what was mentioned with it.

Al-Qurṭubī carries the saying of al-Ḥasan: this saying of theirs was in the wilderness, when they grew weary of the manna and the quails and remembered their first living in Egypt; al-Ḥasan said: they were people of leeks and onions and lentils, and they pulled toward their origin — the origin of evil — and their natures yearned for what their custom had run upon. And the fūm has its readings: Mujāhid said, its fūm — the bread; and it was said, the fūm is the chickpea, in the language of the people of the Levant, and its seller is a fāmī; and ʿAṭāʾ and Qatādah said: the fūm is every grain that is baked. And the town has its question: al-Ṭabarī states the worthiest of its interpretations: there is no indication in the Book of God of the right of these two interpretations, nor a report from the Messenger ﷺ whose coming cuts off the excuse; so the worthiest of the sayings is to say that Mūsā asked his Lord to give his people what they asked, of the plants of the earth, and they were wandering in the earth, and God answered Mūsā in his supplication, and commanded him to go down with those of his people who were with him to a settlement of the earth that would grow for them what he asked for them of that — since what they asked does not grow it except the villages and the towns; and it is possible that that settlement was Egypt, and possible that it was another. The verse measures the exchange by its direction: the better is asked to be exchanged for the lower, and the asking itself is recorded against the askers.

---

## Verse 2:62

> Indeed, the believers, Jews, Christians, and Sabians—whoever ˹truly˺ believes in Allah and the Last Day and does good will have their reward with their Lord. And there will be no fear for them, nor will they grieve

**THE NAMES AND THE ONE CONDITION**

The sūrah now steps back from the history of the house to the law of the Lord over the religions, and it names the four communities: those who believe, and the Jews, and the Christians, and the Ṣābiʾūn. The name of the Jews is from Judah, the son of Yaʿqūb, and the Christians are the Naṣārā: Qatādah, Ibn Jurayj and Ibn ʿAbbās said they were called by the land of al-Nāṣirah where ʿĪsā lived; and the Ṣābiʾūn are the fourth community whose standing the scholars discussed. Mujāhid said, in the report of Layth ibn Abī Sulaym: the Ṣābiʾūn are between the Majūs, the Jews and the Christians, and they have no religion of their own; and similar was reported from Ibn Abī Najīḥ, ʿAṭāʾ and Saʿīd ibn Jubayr; and it is the opinion closest to the truth.

The verse then gives the one condition that stands over the four names: whoever believes in Allah and the Last Day and does the righteous deeds, their reward is with their Lord, and there is no fear upon them nor do they grieve. The condition is the condition of the truth of the names: the name of the religion does not save its carrier — the belief and the deeds are what save. Ibn ʿAbbās said, in the report of ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭalḥah, that Allah revealed after this verse: (3:85 — **“Whoever seeks a way other than Islam, it will never be accepted from them, and in the Hereafter they will be among the losers”**), and his statement indicates that Allah does not accept any deed from anyone unless it conforms to the law of Muhammad ﷺ after Allah sent him. The same promise stands in the sūrah of the Table, with the same names and the same condition: (5:69 — **“Indeed, the believers, Jews, Sabians and Christians—whoever ˹truly˺ believes in Allah and the Last Day and does good, there will be no fear for them, nor will they grieve”**). The verse is the sūrah's standing statement that the house is not saved by its name but by its belief, and the belief that saves after the coming of this Prophet is the belief in what he brought.

**THE NAMES OF THE FOUR**

Al-Ṭabarī reads the four names one by one: as for those who believed, they are the affirmers of the Messenger of God in what he brought them of the truth from God; and as for those who judaised, they are the Jews, and the meaning of they judaised is, they repented — one says of it, the people judaised, a judaising; and it was said that the Jews were named Jews because of their saying, we have turned to You. And Ibn Jurayj said: the Jews were named only because they said, we have turned to You. And the Christians are a gathering, their single is naṣrān, as the single of the drunken is drunkard; except that what is widespread in the speech of the Arabs, for the single of the Christians, is naṣrānī; and it is reported from them, by hearing, naṣrān with the dropping of the yāʾ. And it is mentioned from Ibn ʿAbbās, from a way that is not approved, that he used to say: the Christians were named Christians only because the village of ʿĪsā the son of Maryam was named Nāṣira, and his companions were named the Nazarenes, and it was said of ʿĪsā, the Nazarene.

And the Ṣābians are read in the reports: Ibn Abī Najīḥ said, and Mujāhid the like: the Ṣābians are between the Jews and the Magians — they have no religion. And al-Ḥasan said, in what Ziyād carries to him: the Ṣābians pray toward the qiblah, and pray the five; and he wished to drop the poll-tax from them, and he was informed after that that they worship the angels. And Abū al-ʿĀliyah said: the Ṣābians are a sect of the People of the Book, who read the Psalms; and it reached Abū Jaʿfar al-Rāzī also that the Ṣābians are a people who worship the angels and read the Psalms and pray toward the qiblah.

Al-Qurṭubī gives the verse its grammar of the return: it was reported from Ibn ʿAbbās that His saying, indeed those who believe, and those who judaised — the verse — is abrogated by His saying, whoever seeks a religion other than Islam, it will never be accepted from him; and others said: it is not abrogated — and it is of the one who stands firm upon his belief, of the believers in the Prophet. The verse is the sūrah's standing of the names before the One: the four names are gathered, and the reward is attached not to the name but to the three — the belief in God, and the Last Day, and the righteous deed.

---

## Verse 2:63

> And ˹remember˺ when We took a covenant from you and raised the mountain above you ˹saying˺, “Hold firmly to that ˹Scripture˺ which We have given you and observe its teachings so perhaps you will become mindful ˹of Allah˺.”

**THE COVENANT UNDER THE MOUNTAIN**

The verse recounts the covenant that was taken from the house with its sign. Allah reminded the Children of Israel of the pledges and the covenants He took from them — to believe in Him alone without partner and to follow His messengers — and He states how the covenant was taken: He raised the mount above their heads so that they would affirm the pledge and abide by it with sincerity and seriousness. The mount is al-Ṭūr, as the sūrah of the Heights explains, and this is the tafsīr of Ibn ʿAbbās, Mujāhid, ʿAṭāʾ, ʿIkrimah, al-Ḥasan, al-Ḍaḥḥāk and al-Rabīʿ ibn Anas; and Ibn ʿAbbās said that al-Ṭūr is the mountain that has vegetation on it, and if there is no vegetation on it, it is not called Ṭūr. In the ḥadīth of the trials Ibn ʿAbbās said: when they refused to obey, Allah raised the mountain above their heads so that they would listen. The same raising stands in the sūrah of the Heights: (7:171 — **“And ˹remember˺ when We raised the mountain over them as if it were a cloud and they thought it would fall on them”**).

The word given under the mountain is the word of the covenant: hold firmly to what We have given you, and remember what is in it, so that perhaps you will have taqwā. Al-Ḥasan said that what We have given you is the Torah; Mujāhid read the holding firmly as the strict adherence to it; and Abū al-ʿĀliyah and al-Rabīʿ said that remember what is in it means: read the Torah and implement it. The covenant is thus the taking of the Book with the two hands — the holding of it and the remembering of what is in it, which is the acting by it — and the purpose is the taqwā, which is the same word the sūrah opened with and the same fruit the worship was commanded for. The verse is the deepest picture of the house's covenant: the mountain raised above the heads, the Book given under it, and the command to hold it firmly — and the verse that follows states what the house did with the covenant after the mountain was lifted.

**THE TAKING OF THE COVENANT AND THE RAISING OF THE MOUNTAIN**

Al-Ṭabarī gives the covenant its cause: the mīthāq is the form of the firmness, by an oath or by a pact or by other than that of the firmnesses; and He means by His saying, and when We took your covenant, the covenant that He informed He took from them in His saying, and when We took the covenant of the Children of Israel — that you worship none but God, and to the parents, good. And the cause of the taking of the covenant upon them is, in what Ibn Zayd mentioned: when Mūsā returned from his Lord with the tablets, he said to his people, the Children of Israel: these tablets are in them the Book of God, in them His command with which He commanded you, and His prohibition with which He prohibited you; and they said, who will take it by your saying, you? — no, by God, until we see God openly, until God rises to us and says, this is My Book, take it; and what is it that He does not speak to us as He spoke to you, O Mūsā, and says, this is My Book, take it? And there came a wrath from God, and there came to them a thunderbolt, and it struck them, and they died, all of them; then He revived them.

And the raising of the mountain is carried in the reports. Mujāhid said: Mūsā commanded his people to enter the gate prostrating and to say the remission, and the gate was lowered for them so that they might prostrate, and they did not prostrate, and they entered upon their backs, and they said, wheat; and the mountain was raised above them — he says, the root of the mountain was brought out from the earth and raised above them as a canopy — and the Ṭūr, in the Syriac, is the mountain — as a frightening, and they entered prostrating upon fear, and their eyes toward the mountain; it is the mountain upon which his Lord manifested Himself to him. And in his other report: the mountain was raised above them as a cloud, and it was said to them, you will believe, or it will fall upon you; and they believed; and the mountain, in the Syriac, is the Ṭūr. And Abū al-ʿĀliyah said: the mountain was raised above them, frightening them with it. And Ibn ʿAbbās said: the Ṭūr is the mountain upon which the Torah was sent down, upon Mūsā; and the Children of Israel were beneath it; and ʿAṭāʾ said: the mountain was raised over the Children of Israel, and it was said, you will believe in it, or it will fall upon you; and that is His saying, as if it were a canopy. And al-Ḍaḥḥāk carries from Ibn ʿAbbās the Ṭūr's own definition: the Ṭūr, of the mountains, is what grows; and what does not grow is not a Ṭūr.

---

## Verse 2:64

> Yet you turned away afterwards. Had it not been for Allah’s grace and mercy upon you, you would have certainly been of the losers

**THE TURNING AND THE MERCY**

The verse states what followed the covenant under the mountain: then you turned away after that. The turning is stated after the firm pledge: after the mountain was raised and the word was given, the house turned away, and the turning is the breaking of the pledge that was taken under the sign. The verse then states what stood between the turning and the loss: had it not been for the favour of Allah upon you and His mercy, you would have been among the losers. The loss was the right of the turning, and the favour and the mercy were what stood in its way, and the verse names the two without specifying them because the whole history of the house is their explanation: the prophets sent after the turning, the repentance accepted after the falls, and the Books renewed after the forgetting.

Abū al-ʿĀliyah said, in the report of al-Rabīʿ, that the favour of Allah here is Islam and His mercy is the Qurʾān — and his reading states the last of the favours that stood between the house and the loss: the Book that is being read over the house in this address is itself the favour and the mercy that followed the turning. The same standing of the favour and the mercy between the servant and the loss is stated in the sūrah of the Light: (24:21 — **“Had it not been for Allah’s grace and mercy upon you, none of you would have ever been purified”**). The verse is the pattern the whole address is built on: the covenant is taken, the turning follows, and the mercy stands between the turning and the loss — and the mercy is the standing argument of the verse: the house that turned away is the house that is still being addressed, and the address itself is the proof that the mercy has not been lifted. The same breaking of the covenant and its fruits stand in the sūrah of the Table: (5:13 — **“But for breaking their covenant We condemned them and hardened their hearts. They distorted the words of the Scripture and neglected a portion of what they had been commanded to uphold”**).

**THE TURNING AWAY AND ITS LANGUAGE**

Al-Ṭabarī reads the turning away by its language: then you turned away — then you turned your backs; and it is only of the form of your saying, so-and-so turned me his back, when he turned from him and left him behind his back; then that is used of every one who leaves an obedience he was commanded to, and turns his face away; one says, so-and-so turned away from the obedience of so-and-so, and turned away from his company. And from that is the saying of God, but when He gave them of His bounty, they were stingy with it and turned away, while they were turning aside. And it is of the way of the Arabs to borrow the word and to place it in the place of its like, as Abū Kharāsh al-Hudhalī said: it is not as the covenant of the dwelling, O Umm Mālik — but the chains have encompassed the necks — he means by the chains that Islam became, in its preventing them of what they used to do in the Ignorance, of what God forbade them in Islam, in the place of the chains encompassing the necks, which come between the one in whose neck they are, with the shackle in his hand, and what he tried to reach of it. So likewise His saying, then you turned away, after that — He means by it that you left the acting upon what We took your covenant and your pacts upon, of the acting upon it with seriousness and striving, after your giving your Lord the covenants upon the acting upon it, and the standing by what He commanded you of it in your Book — and you cast it behind your backs. And He makes a hidden reference, by His saying, that, to all that is before it in the preceding verse — His saying, and when We took your covenant, and We raised above you the mountain. The verse binds the turning away to the mountain: the covenant was taken under the raised canopy, and the turning away came after the canopy — and the grace and the mercy of the verse stand between the turning and the loss.

---

## Verse 2:65

> You are already aware of those of you who broke the Sabbath. We said to them, “Be disgraced apes!”

**THE TOWN BY THE SEA**

The verse turns to the punishment the house already knows: and you have known those among you who transgressed in the matter of the Sabbath. The story is the story of the town by the sea, which the sūrah of the Heights recounts in full: (7:163 — **“Ask them ˹O Prophet˺ about ˹the people of˺ the town which was by the sea, who broke the Sabbath. During the Sabbath, ˹abundant˺ fish would come to them clearly visible, but on other days the fish were never seen. In this way We tested them for their rebelliousness”**). The trial of the town was the trial of the fish: they came openly on the day of the Sabbath and they did not come on the other days, and the people of the town used to rebel, and they hunted the fish of the Sabbath by the devices that made the forbidden appear as the permitted.

The punishment of the transgression is stated in the verse: We said to them, be monkeys, despised. Ibn ʿAbbās said, in the report of al-ʿAwfī: Allah changed their bodies into the bodies of monkeys and swine — the young became monkeys and the old became swine; and Qatādah said, in the report of Shaybān al-Naḥwī: they were turned into howling monkeys with tails, after being men and women. The reports state that the transformation was the end of their kind: Ibn Abī Ḥātim recorded that Ibn ʿAbbās said that those who violated the Sabbath were turned into monkeys and then perished without offspring; and al-Ḍaḥḥāk reported from Ibn ʿAbbās that they lived only three days, for no transformed one lives more than three days, and they did not eat or drink or have offspring. The warning of their punishment stands in the word of the Prophet ﷺ that Imam Abū ʿAbd Allāh ibn Baṭṭah recorded from Abū Hurayrah: do not commit what the Jews committed, breaching what Allah has forbidden by resorting to the lowest types of deceit. The verse is the standing warning against the devices of the forbidden: the fish of the Sabbath are still coming on their day, and the devices are still being made, and the transformation that struck the town stands over every device as its end.

**THE SABBATH AND ITS NAME, AND THE MONKEYS**

Al-Ṭabarī gives the Sabbath its root: the root of the Sabbath is the settling and the stillness, in rest and ease; and for that it is said of the sleeper, masbūt, for his settling and the stillness of his body and his rest — as God said (78:9 — **“and made your sleep for rest”**), that is, a rest for your bodies; and it is a source from the saying of the speaker, so-and-so sabbathed, he sabbaths, a sabbathing. And it was said that it was named a Sabbath because God finished, on the Friday — and it is the day before it — from the creation of all His creation. And of the despised, he says: the one removed, driven away, as the dog is driven away; one says of it, I drove him away, I drive him away, a driving — and the meaning of His saying, be monkeys, despised, is, ones removed from the good, abased, made small; and Mujāhid said: abased.

Ibn ʿAbbās gives the Sabbath its story, in the report of ʿIkrimah: God imposed upon the Children of Israel only the day He imposed upon you in your festival — the Friday — and they differed to the Sabbath and honoured it, and left what they were commanded; and when they refused but the keeping of the Sabbath, God tried them in it, and forbade them in it what He made lawful to them in other than it; and they were in a village between Aylah and the Ṭūr, called Madyan; and God forbade them in the Sabbath the fish — the catching of them and the eating of them; and when it was the day of the Sabbath, the fish came to them, openly, to the shore of their sea, until when the Sabbath was gone, they went, and they saw no fish, small or great.

And Kathīr carries the report of the transformation: Allah changed their bodies into those of monkeys and swines — the young became monkeys, and the old became swine; and Shaybān al-Naḥwī reports that Qatādah commented on be monkeys, despised: these people were transformed into howling monkeys with tails, after they had been men and women. And the standing answer of the verse is carried with it: the monkeys and the swine that exist now are not of the descendants of those who were transformed.

---

## Verse 2:66

> So We made their fate an example to present and future generations, and a lesson to the God-fearing

**THE EXAMPLE AND THE LESSON**

The verse states the standing of the punishment after it: We made it an example for those in front of it and those behind it, and a lesson for the muttaqīn. The example is the nakāl, the punishment that is made a sign, and Ibn ʿAbbās read it of the town and its neighbours: We made this town an example for the villages around it by the manner in which We punished its people, as the Book states of the towns around the address: (46:27 — **“We certainly destroyed the societies around you after having varied the signs so perhaps they would return ˹to the Right Path˺”**). The example is for the ones before it and the ones behind it — the generations that saw it and the generations that heard it — and the verse of the sūrah is itself part of the example: the story that is being read over the community of Muhammad ﷺ is the standing of the punishment as the sign.

The verse then names the ones for whom the sign is the lesson: and a lesson for the muttaqīn. The lesson is the reading of the punishment by the ones who fear the One who sent it: the torment that struck the town was the fruit of indulging in the prohibitions of Allah and the devices against them, and the muttaqīn are warned by it from the like of its behaviour, so that what befell it does not befall them. The verse closes the story of the Sabbath on its two fruits: the example for the generations and the lesson for the God-fearing, and the two are the two readings of the one punishment — the reading of the eye and the reading of the heart.

**WHAT THE PRONOUN RETURNS UPON**

The people of interpretation differed over the pronoun of so We made it — upon what does it return? Ibn ʿAbbās said, in the report of al-Ḍaḥḥāk: so We made it — We made that punishment — and it is the transformation — a deterrent; so the pronoun is, upon this saying of Ibn ʿAbbās, a hidden reference to the transformation, and the meaning of the speech is: We said to them, be monkeys, despised, and they became transformed monkeys, and We made Our punishment of them, and Our transformation of them, a deterrent for what is before it and what is behind it, and an admonition for the God-fearing. And Ibn ʿAbbās said, in his other report: so We made it — he means, the fish; and the pronoun, upon this saying, is of the mention of the fish, and no mention of them had run, but since there is in the report an indication, the hidden reference to their mention is made, and the indication upon that is His saying, and you have known those of you who transgressed in the Sabbath. And others said: We made it — the village whose people transgressed in the Sabbath; so the pronoun, in the saying of these, is a hidden reference to the village of the people who were transformed.

And the deterrent is read by its meaning: al-Ḍaḥḥāk carries from Ibn ʿAbbās: a deterrent — he says, a punishment; and al-Rabīʿ said: a deterrent, that is, a punishment. And of the two sides, Ibn ʿAbbās said: for what is before it — he says, that the one after them may beware of My punishment; and what is behind it — he says, those who remained with them. The verse makes the one punishment three readings: the deterrent for what is before it, and the deterrent for what is behind it, and the admonition for the God-fearing — and the punishment that struck one village is made to stand over the villages all.

---

## Verse 2:67

> And ˹remember˺ when Moses said to his people, “Allah commands you to sacrifice a cow.” They replied, “Are you mocking us?” Moses responded, “I seek refuge in Allah from acting foolishly!”

**THE COMMAND OF THE COW**

The sūrah now turns to the story that carries its name, and it opens with the command. Mūsā said to his people: Allah commands you to slaughter a cow. The command is given in its simplest form — a cow, any cow — and the early scholars stated the measure of its simplicity: Ibn ʿAbbās and ʿUbaydah said that had they slaughtered a cow, any cow, it would have been sufficient for them, but they made the matter difficult, so Allah made it difficult for them. The story behind the command is the story of the murdered man of the Children of Israel, whose killer was hidden, and the cow was the means by which the murderer was to be made known and the murdered man brought back to life — the miracle that the verse will close upon.

The answer of the house to the command is the first of its answers: they said, do you take us in mockery? The question of the house returns the command upon its carrier: the prophet who brings the command of Allah is asked whether he is mocking, and the answer of Mūsā is the prophet's refuge: I seek refuge in Allah from being among the ignorant. The mocking is the act of the ignorant, and the prophet takes refuge with Allah from the standing that would make the command a mockery. The verse opens the story of the cow on the question that will run through it: the house asks and asks, and the command narrows with every question, and the narrowing is the fruit of the asking — and the verse that follows begins the first of the questions. The murder that stands behind the command is the standing matter of the Book, and the first murder of the sons of Adam is recounted in the sūrah of the Table: (5:27 — **“Relate to them in truth ˹O Prophet˺ the story of Adam’s two sons—how each offered a sacrifice”**).

**THE SLAIN MAN AND THE COW**

Al-Ṭabarī gives the verse its place: this verse is of what God reproached with it the addressed of the Children of Israel, for the breaking of their ancients of the covenant God took upon them, of the obedience to His prophets; and He said to them: remember also, of your breaking of My covenant, when Mūsā said to his people — and his people are the Children of Israel, when they disputed over the slain man who was killed among them, and pushed it to him: indeed God commands you to slaughter a cow; they said, do you take us in mockery? And the huzuʾ is the play and the derision. And it does not befit that there be, of the prophets of God, in what they report of God, of a command or a prohibition, mockery or play; and they supposed of Mūsā, in his commanding them — by the command of God — with the slaughtering of the cow, at their disputing over the slain man and pushing it to him, that he was mocking, playing; and it was not for them to suppose that of the prophet of God, while he was informing them that God is the one who commanded them with the slaughtering of the cow. And Mūsā informed them, when they said to him what they said, that the reporter of God, with mockery and derision, is of the ignorant; and he cleared himself of what they supposed of him, and said, I take refuge in God from being of the ignorant — meaning, of the fools, who report lies and falsehood of God.

Al-Qurṭubī gives the story its slain man: that was because they found a slain man among them — it was said that his name was ʿĀmil — and the affair of his killer was doubtful to them, and disagreement fell between them, and they said, we fight while the Messenger of God is among us; and they came to him and asked him the making plain — and that was before the descent of the oath. And Kathīr carries the report of ʿUbaydah al-Salmānī: there was a man of the Children of Israel who was childless, and he had great wealth, and he had only a nephew who would inherit from him; and his nephew killed him and moved his body by night and placed him at the door of a man; and in the morning the nephew cried out for blood, and the people took up their weapons and nearly fought one another; and the wise among them said, why do you kill one another, while the Messenger of God is among you? — and they came to him; and had they not disputed, any cow would have sufficed them, but they disputed, and the affair was made harder upon them, until they ended to seeking the cow that was described; and they found the designated cow with a man who owned only that cow, and he said, by God, I will not sell it but for the fill of its skin with gold; and they paid the fill of its skin with gold, and they slaughtered it, and they touched the dead man with a part of it, and he stood up, and they asked him.

---

## Verse 2:68

> They said, “Call upon your Lord to clarify for us what type ˹of cow˺ it should be!” He replied, “Allah says, ‘The cow should neither be old nor young but in between. So do as you are commanded!’”

**THE FIRST QUESTION AND ITS ANSWER**

The verse records the first question of the house: they said, call upon your Lord for us, that He may make clear to us what she is. The question asks for the description of the cow the command left general, and the word of the question — call upon your Lord — is the word that will repeat through the story: the Lord is named in the question as your Lord, as if the Lord of the command were the Lord of Mūsā alone and not their Lord with him. The answer comes through the prophet: He says: it is a cow neither farid nor bikr — neither old nor young — but ʿawān between that. The early scholars read the three words as the one description: Abū al-ʿĀliyah, al-Suddī, Mujāhid, ʿIkrimah, ʿAṭāʾ, Wahb ibn Munabbih, al-Ḍaḥḥāk, al-Ḥasan, Qatādah and Ibn ʿAbbās said that the cow is neither old nor below the age of breeding, and al-Ḍaḥḥāk reported from Ibn ʿAbbās that ʿawān between that means she was at the age when the cow is strongest and fittest.

The answer closes on the command that the question should have ended: so do what you are commanded. The word is the prophet's counsel at the end of the answer: the description has been given, and the remaining duty is the doing, and the doing is what the first command asked for. The verse is the standing lesson of the story: the command of Allah is light while it is general, and the questions of the servant are what narrow it — and the house that asked for the description received it with the command to stop asking and begin doing. The same counsel of the doing over the asking stands in the sūrah of the Women: (4:66 — **“Had they done what they were advised to do, it would have certainly been far better for them and more reassuring”**).

**THE FIRST QUESTION AND ITS PUNISHMENT**

Al-Ṭabarī reads the question as the punishment's own cause: those to whom it was said, indeed God commands you to slaughter a cow, said, after they knew, and it was settled with them, that what Mūsā commanded them of that, by the command of God, of the slaughtering of a cow, was earnest and truth: call upon your Lord for us, that He make plain to us what she is; and they asked Mūsā to ask his Lord, for them, what God had sufficed them of, by His saying to them, slaughter a cow — for He commanded them only with the slaughtering of a cow of the cows, whichever cow they wished to slaughter, without restricting that to a kind of it rather than a kind, or a class rather than a class; and they said, by the roughness of their characters, and the coarseness of their natures, and the evil of their understandings, and the taking upon themselves of what God had put off them. And Ibn ʿAbbās said: when Mūsā said to them, I take refuge in God from being of the ignorant, they said to him, pressing him hard, call upon your Lord for us, that He make plain to us what she is.

And when they took upon themselves, in ignorance, what they took upon themselves, of the search into what they had been sufficed of, of the description of the cow they were commanded to slaughter, pressing their prophet Mūsā hard, after what they had shown him of the evil of their opinion of him, in what he informed them of, from God, by their saying, do you take us in mockery — He punished them by restricting the slaughter of what He commanded them to slaughter, of the cows, to a kind of it rather than a kind. Al-Qurṭubī states the rule: this is a pressing of them, and a lack of compliance; and had they complied with the command and slaughtered any cow at all, the purpose would have been obtained, but they made it hard upon themselves, and God made it hard upon them — Ibn ʿAbbās and Abū al-ʿĀliyah and others said it. And he draws from it the doctrine: in this is an evidence of the permissibility of the abrogation before the time of the act — for when He commanded a cow, it required any cow at all, and when He added the description, He abrogated the first ruling by another, as when one says, of thirty of the camels, a she-camel, then abrogates it by another. And the neither old is read: not an old one, worn out — one says of it, the cow became old, she becomes old. The verse records the first tightening, and the verses that follow will record the rest — and each question is written down as the cause of the next straitness.

---

## Verse 2:69

> They said, “Call upon your Lord to specify for us its colour.” He replied, “Allah says, ‘It should be a bright yellow cow—pleasant to see.’”

**THE SECOND QUESTION AND THE COLOUR**

The verse records the second question of the house: they said, call upon your Lord for us, that He may make clear to us what her colour is. The first question asked for the kind, and the second asks for the colour, and the asking continues where the command had asked for the doing. The answer comes with the colour: He says: it is a yellow cow, fāqiʿ its colour — and the early scholars read the fāqiʿ as the intensity of the yellow: a deep, bright yellow that fills the eye. The description is completed with the pleasure of the seeing: it pleases the beholders, and Abū al-ʿĀliyah, Qatādah and al-Rabīʿ ibn Anas said that it pleases those who see it; and Wahb ibn Munabbih said: if you look at the cow's skin, you will think that the sun's rays radiate through its skin.

The verse gives the colour of the cow and the effect of the colour together: the yellow that is bright and the beauty that pleases, so that the cow described is known by two signs — its colour and its effect on the eye. The house that asked for the colour received the colour that none could miss, and the asking was bringing the command closer to the one cow and farther from the any cow — and the verse that follows records the third question, which is the last before the doing. The stories of the Book are carried for their lessons, as the sūrah of Yūsuf states of them: (12:111 — **“In their stories there is truly a lesson for people of reason”**).

**THE COLOUR AND THE READINGS OF THE YELLOW**

Al-Ṭabarī reads the second question as the second pressing: the people of Mūsā said to Mūsā: call upon your Lord for us, that He make plain to us what is her colour — that is, what is the colour of the cow you commanded us to slaughter; and this also is another pressing of theirs, after the first, and a taking upon themselves of the seeking of what they had been sufficed of, in the second time and the later question. And they had not been restricted, in the second time, when it was said to them, after their question of the outfitting of the cow they were commanded to slaughter — and they refused but the taking upon themselves of what they had been sufficed of, of the question of her description — so they were restricted to a kind rather than the rest of the kinds, as a punishment from God upon them, for their question which they asked their prophet ﷺ, pressing him hard; then He did not restrict them to a colour of it rather than a colour, and they refused but the taking upon themselves of what they had been sufficed of.

And the yellow is read in the reports. Some said: its meaning is, black, intense of blackness; and al-Ḥasan said: yellow, intense of its colour — black, intense of blackness; and in another report from al-Ḥasan: yellow of the horn and the hoof. And al-Qurṭubī states the soundest: the majority of the commentators are that she is yellow of colour, of the known yellowness; and Makki reports from some: until the horn and the hoof; and al-Ḥasan and Ibn Jubayr said: she was yellow of the horn and the hoof only. And he answers the blackness: the first is sounder, because it is the apparent, and this is anomalous, not used figuratively except of the camels; and had He intended the blackness, He would not have stressed it with the brightness, for that is a description particular to the yellowness, and the blackness is not described with it. And of the pleasing to the beholders, he says: He means, pure is her colour, no colour in her other than the colour of her skin; and Wahb said: it is as if the ray of the sun comes out of her skin; and for that Ibn ʿAbbās said: the yellowness pleases the soul; and ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib said: whoever wears two sandals of yellow skin, his grief is lessened, because God said, yellow, bright is her colour, pleasing to the beholders. The colour of the verse is the sign that narrows the search to one cow in the world — and the brightness is the mark by which the one is known.

---

## Verse 2:70

> Again they said, “Call upon your Lord so that He may make clear to us which cow, for all cows look the same to us. Then, Allah willing, we will be guided ˹to the right one˺.”

**THE LAST QUESTION AND THE GUIDANCE**

The verse records the third and last of the questions: they said, call upon your Lord for us, that He may make clear to us what she is, for the cows have become alike to us, and we, if Allah wills, are among the guided. The question confesses its own cause: the cows have become alike to them — the descriptions that were given have gathered the command onto one cow, and the likeness of the other cows is what remains between them and the finding of it. The verse closes the asking on the word of the guidance: and we, if Allah wills, are among the guided, and the istithnāʾ is the house's return to the Lord of the command in the last of its questions.

The early scholars read the whole asking as the making of the difficulty. Ibn ʿAbbās said, in the report of Saʿīd ibn Jubayr: had they taken the nearest cow, it would have sufficed them, but they made it difficult, so Allah made it difficult for them; and ʿIkrimah said: had the Children of Israel taken a cow, it would have sufficed them, and were it not for their saying, if Allah wills, we are among the guided, they would not have found it; and Mujāhid read the same reading through the three questions: had they taken a cow of any description, it would have sufficed them. The verse is the standing lesson of the story for the community that reads it: the command is found in the doing, and the asking that delays the doing is the asking that narrows the command — and the word of the guidance at the end of the questions is the word that saved them the finding of the cow. The community of this Book is taught the same law of the asking in the sūrah of the Table: (5:101 — **“O believers! Do not ask about any matter which, if made clear to you, may disturb you”**).

**THE THIRD IGNORANCE AND THE EXCEPTION**

Al-Ṭabarī reads the verse by its numbering: He means by His saying, they said — the people of Mūsā, who were commanded with the slaughtering of the cow, said to Mūsā; and the mention of Mūsā is left, and the mention of the returning of his mention, by the sufficiency of what the apparent of the speech indicated of it. And His saying, that He make plain to us what she is, is a report from God of the people, of a third ignorance of theirs — for had they, when they were commanded with the slaughtering of the cow, slaughtered any one that came easily, of what the name of cow falls upon, it would have sufficed them, and there would have been nothing upon them other than it, for they had not been imposed upon it by a description rather than a description; and when they asked the making plain of her, by what description she is, it was made plain to them that she was of an age of the ages, rather than the ages of the rest of the ages, and it was said to them, she is between the old and the young and the weak — and they were, when her age was made plain to them, had they slaughtered the nearest cow of the age that was made plain to them, it would have sufficed them. But the people, when they increased their prophet Mūsā ﷺ in harm and pressing, God increased them in punishment and tightening.

Ibn ʿAbbās said: had they taken the nearest cow, they would have been sufficed by it, but they made it hard, and God made it hard upon them; and ʿUbaydah said: had they taken the nearest cow, it would have sufficed them. And the Messenger of God ﷺ gives the verse its standing rule, in what Abū Hurayrah carries: leave me, as long as I leave you — for those before you were destroyed only by the abundance of their questioning, and their differing upon their prophets; and when I command you with a thing, then come to it, and when I forbid you a thing, then leave it, as much as you are able.

Al-Qurṭubī reads the fourth question and the exception: the cows are confused upon us — they asked a fourth question, and they did not comply with the command after the making plain; and they mentioned the cows because it is in the meaning of the gathering, and for that He said, the cows are confused upon us, and made it masculine, for the masculinity of the wording of the cows; and al-Zajjāj said: the meaning is, the kind of the cows. And of His saying, and we, if God wills, are guided — it is an exception from them; and in their exception, in this last question, is some returning and some compliance, and an evidence of regret upon the non-conformity to the command; and it is reported from the Prophet ﷺ that he said: had they not excepted, they would not have been guided to her, ever. The verse closes the cow's story with the exception: the questioning ended where the submission began, and the guidance of the verse was the fruit of the if God wills.

---

## Verse 2:71

> He replied, “Allah says, ‘It should have been used neither to till the soil nor water the fields; wholesome and without blemish.’” They said, “Now you have come with the truth.” Yet they still slaughtered it hesitantly

**THE LAST DESCRIPTIONS AND THE SLAUGHTER**

The verse completes the description of the cow with its last two signs. The first is the sign of the work: it is a cow not trained to till the soil nor to water the fields — she is not used in farming or in watering, and the early scholars read the description as the honour of the cow: rather she is honourable and fair-looking. The second is the sign of the soundness: musallamah, free of defects. Qatādah said, in the report of Maʿmar from ʿAbd al-Razzāq, that musallamah means the cow does not suffer from any defects, and this is also the opinion of Abū al-ʿĀliyah and al-Rabīʿ; and Mujāhid said the same, and ʿAṭāʾ al-Khurāsānī said it means that its legs and body are free of physical defects. The cow is thus described by five signs: the age between the two, the brightness of the yellow, the pleasure of the beholders, the freedom from the work, and the freedom from the defects — and the five are the five narrowings that the questions brought upon the command.

The house answers the completed description with its word: now you have come with the truth — the truth of the description that leaves no likeness between this cow and the other cows. The verse then states the doing: so they slaughtered it, and they almost did not do it. Al-Ḍaḥḥāk reported from Ibn ʿAbbās that it means they did not want to slaughter it; and ʿUbaydah, Mujāhid, Wahb ibn Munabbih, Abū al-ʿĀliyah and ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Zayd said that the Jews bought the cow with a large amount of money. The verse closes the asking on the reluctance: after all the questions and the answers, the house was still reluctant to slaughter the cow, because the goal of the asking was not the doing but the delaying. The slaughter leads to the miracle the verses close upon: (2:73 — **“This is how ˹easily˺ Allah brings the dead to life, showing you His signs so that you may understand”**).

**THE LAST DESCRIPTION AND THE NEARLY**

Al-Ṭabarī reads the last description: Mūsā said: God says that the cow I commanded you to slaughter is a cow not made lowly — that is, work has not made her lowly; so the meaning of the verse is: she is a cow whose plowing of the earth with her hooves has not made her lowly, nor has water been drawn upon her and the crop been watered upon her; as it is said of the beast that riding or work has made lowly, a beast lowly, manifest of the lowness; and of the like of it, of the sons of Ādam, a man lowly, manifest of the lowness and the lowliness. And Qatādah said: not made lowly — he says, difficult, work has not made her lowly, plowing the earth, and not watering the tillage. And al-Suddī said: not made lowly, plowing the earth — he says, a cow that is not lowly, cultivated upon, and she does not water the tillage. And Abū al-ʿĀliyah said: not made lowly — that is, work has not made her lowly; plowing the earth — he means, she is not lowly so that she plows the earth; and not watering the tillage — he says, and she does not work in the tillage.

The descriptions of the verse stand together as the narrowing of the search to one cow in all the earth: not old and not young, between that; yellow, bright of colour, pleasing to the beholders; not made lowly, neither plowing the earth nor watering the tillage, without blemish. And the verse closes with the nearly: so they slaughtered her, and they nearly did not do it — for the questions had brought the command to the edge of the leaving, and the slaughtering came as the obedience that remained after the stripping of every excuse.

---

## Verse 2:72

> ˹This is˺ when a man was killed and you disputed who the killer was, but Allah revealed what you concealed

**THE DISPUTE OVER THE KILLED MAN**

The verse gives the story behind the command of the cow. Remember when you killed a man and disputed among yourselves about the crime. The word of the dispute is the word of the pushing away: faddāraʾtum, you pushed the crime from one to another, and al-Bukhārī said of the word that it means you disputed, and this is also the tafsīr of Mujāhid, and ʿAṭāʾ al-Khurāsānī and al-Ḍaḥḥāk said: you disputed about the matter. Ibn Jurayj said that some of them said, you killed him, and the others said, no, you killed him — and this is also the tafsīr of ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Zayd ibn Aslam. The reports give the story of the killed man: he was an old man of the Children of Israel who was killed by his nephews for his inheritance, and they threw him at the door of another quarter and each party accused the other of his killing, and the dispute was carried to Mūsā.

The verse then states what the dispute could not hide: and Allah was bringing forth what you were concealing. Mujāhid said it means what you were hiding, and the bringing forth is the act of the verse's Lord: the killing was concealed by the dispute, and the concealment was brought forth by the command of the cow. The verse is the standing proof of the story: the cow was not commanded for its own sake — the cow was the means by which the concealed was to be brought forth, and the means of the miracle was chosen from the thing the house had made difficult. The murder that stands behind the verse is the standing matter of the Book: the killing of the soul that Allah has forbidden, and the dispute that tries to bury it, and the Lord who brings forth what the disputants conceal. Nothing of the affair is hidden from Him, as the sūrah of the Ants states: (27:75 — **“For there is nothing hidden in the heavens or the earth without being ˹written˺ in a perfect Record”**).

**THE DISPUTING AND THE LANGUAGE OF THE DARʾ**
